CHICAGO, ILLINOIS - AUGUST 23: Kelsey Mitchell #0 and Caitlin Clark #22 of the Indiana Fever confer against the Chicago Sky during the first half at Wintrust Arena on August 23, 2026 in Chicago, Illinois. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Michael Reaves/Getty Images) Getty

The Indiana Fever are moving closer to finding their next basketball executive, and their search has drawn three candidates with extensive NBA experience.

Boston Celtics vice president Allison Feaster, Sacramento Kings vice president Phil Jabour and former Dallas Mavericks executive Matt Riccardi have emerged as strong candidates for the position, according to Annie Costabile and Sam Amick of The Athletic, who first reported the development Tuesday, Nov. 17.

ESPN's Alexa Philippou and Shams Charania subsequently reported that Indiana is working through a group of finalists, with a hire expected within the next month.

Philippou also clarified that the Fever intend to hire one basketball operations executive rather than separate replacements for former team president Kelly Krauskopf and former general manager Amber Cox.

The search comes at a critical point for Indiana, which must address major roster decisions surrounding Caitlin Clark and Kelsey Mitchell.

The development comes nearly four weeks after the Fever parted ways with two of their highest-ranking executives.

Getty13 Jun 2000: Allison Feaster of the Los Angeles Sparks dribbles the ball during a game against the Seattle Storm at Key Arena in Seattle, Washington. The Storm defeated the Sparks 59-69.

Feaster brings a combination of playing experience and NBA front office leadership.

A former WNBA player, she currently serves as Boston's vice president of team operations and organizational growth. She joined the Celtics in 2019 after an accomplished professional playing career.

Her work has focused on player development, organizational culture and relationships throughout the basketball operation.

The Kings executive has worked extensively in scouting, player evaluation and roster construction. Before joining Sacramento, he held a scouting leadership position with the Philadelphia 76ers.

Riccardi, meanwhile, previously served as an assistant general manager with the Mavericks.

He also spent more than a decade with the Brooklyn Nets organization, including a stint running their G League affiliate.

Following Nico Harrison's dismissal in Dallas in November 2025, Riccardi and Michael Finley briefly shared interim general manager responsibilities.

All three candidates bring NBA experience, although Indiana has not announced a finalist or identified a preferred choice.

The Fever's search began after team president Kelly Krauskopf stepped down Oct. 23.

General manager and chief operating officer Amber Cox departed later that day, leaving Indiana with significant vacancies in basketball leadership.

However, Philippou clarified that the Fever intend to hire one basketball executive rather than separate replacements for Krauskopf and Cox.

The incoming executive will inherit a franchise with championship expectations and several consequential personnel decisions approaching.

Krauskopf helped build the Fever from their inception and guided the franchise through its first WNBA championship in 2012.

Cox, meanwhile, oversaw basketball operations during a period of heightened attention surrounding the team's young core.

GettyKelsey Mitchell and Caitlin Clark of the Indiana Fever confer against the Chicago Sky during the first half at Wintrust Arena on August 23, 2026 in Chicago, Illinois.

The timing of Indiana's search adds urgency to the decision.

Caitlin Clark remains the centerpiece of the franchise, while Kelsey Mitchell enters another important offseason following a career-best 2026 campaign.

Mitchell's upcoming free agency presents an immediate challenge for whoever takes charge.

The four-time All-Star has established herself as one of the WNBA's premier scorers, making her future a central question in Indiana's plans.

The next executive must also evaluate the roster around Clark and Mitchell while working with coach Stephanie White to establish a sustainable championship contender.

Indiana's leadership changes have created uncertainty. Tuesday's report, however, suggests the search is entering its final stages.

With several NBA-connected candidates under consideration and a decision expected within weeks, the Fever could soon have the person responsible for guiding their next chapter.

#2789. LeBron James sues startup over viral AI ad allegedly exploiting his likeness

NBA superstar LeBron James sued artificial intelligence-powered sports betting startup Bron and its two founders, accusing them of using his name, likeness and AI-generated voice without permission to promote their app.

In a lawsuit filed Tuesday in the U.S. District Court for the Southern District of New York, James accused PlayVision, which operates the Bron app, and co-founders Anish Gupta and Marc Zoghby of deliberately exploiting his "Bron" nickname to mislead consumers into believing he endorsed or was affiliated with the app.

The complaint alleges that the startup released an AI-generated advertisement earlier this month depicting James in a basketball locker room, wearing a jersey bearing his nickname and the iconic number 23, along with his tattoos and a simulated version of his voice.

The video showed the AI-generated figure scrolling through NBA betting options on the app.

James alleged that the defendants deliberately sought to provoke controversy to generate publicity for their app, with the advertisement attracting about one million views on X within a day.

The lawsuit claims the founders calculated that the publicity would outweigh any potential legal consequences, effectively treating the alleged violation of James' rights as a marketing expense.

It argues that James' endorsements are "uniquely valuable," adding, "That is why defendants decided to steal it for themselves."

The NBA star's lawyers sent a cease-and-desist letter on Nov. 5, after which the startup removed the ad from its X and Instagram accounts and said it was working to change the app's name, according to the complaint.

However, the ad remained available through third-party reposts, and the company had not changed the app's name as of the lawsuit's filing.

James is seeking damages and a court order preventing the defendants from using his name, image, likeness, and voice, as well as the Bron name.

Bron, operated by PlayVision, is described in Apple's App Store as a "sports analytics and betting research app with live odds comparisons, data-backed picks, player and team trends, a multi-leg Slip and bet tracking." The listing says it does not accept wagers.

PlayVision is headquartered in California and participated in Y Combinator's fall 2025 batch.

— CNBC's Dan Mangan contributed to this story.

#2788. NFL flexes Raiders-Browns to NBC's Sunday Night Football, Patriots-Chargers out

NFL flexes Raiders-Browns to NBC's Sunday Night Football, Patriots-Chargers out originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The league on Tuesday flexed the Las Vegas Raiders-Cleveland Browns Week 12 matchup into NBC's Sunday Night Football slot. It was initially slated for a 1 p.m. ET start.

The New England Patriots–Los Angeles Chargers game that was initially set for primetime will move up to a 4:05 p.m. ET kickoff and will be on CBS.

The NFL can flex games up to 12 days before they are scheduled, though Week 12 caused rarer challenges due to Thanksgiving week featuring more primetime games than usual.

Despite making the Super Bowl last season, the Patriots have regressed after a 5-5 start. Quarterback Drake Maye is also now set for an extended spell on the sidelines, causing further issues for an ailing team.

The Chargers have been one of the surprises of the season, but for all the wrong reasons. Justin Herbert and Co. went winless for their first eight games. They just finally took down a Jacoby Brissett-led Baltimore team to get to 1-8.

Las Vegas will enter the matchup with No. 1 overall pick Fernando Mendoza taking over from Kirk Cousins, who was traded to Minnesota on deadline day. The 4-6 Raiders will be on primetime for the first time this season. Their last SNF game was in 2023.

Cleveland is 6-4 and currently occupies the final AFC playoff spot as Deshaun Watson returns to form. The Browns are 4-0 at home for the first time since 1963, though they've eked out several close wins. Cleveland last hosted an SNF game in 2019.

Its most recent SNF game was in 2021 at Baltimore, and its only other primetime game this season was a Thursday night Week 4 home win vs. Pittsburgh.

The league also announced the Tennessee Titans-Jacksonville Jaguars game will move up to 1 p.m. ET.

#2786. 'Who and what comes next': Analysts expect Nvidia investor uncertainty despite earnings beat

Analysts expect uncertainty around Nvidia's future to linger despite another earnings beat, noting that much of the third quarter was already locked in before the turmoil that peaked in Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO.

Nvidia's third-quarter revenue came in at $115.84 billion, more than $6 billion above analysts' expectations of $109.10 billion, while fourth-quarter guidance of $130 billion topped the $125.1 billion consensus, according to LSEG.

Part of that increase reflects an extra week in the fiscal fourth quarter, which Melius Research estimated accounts for $9 billion to $10 billion of the sequential jump.

The company also lifted its fiscal 2028 growth forecast to 75% from 70%, even with China excluded from its data center compute revenue outlook.

"That's a team going full steam ahead, rather than just steadying the ship, and a clear signal that management believes next year can run just fine without Jensen Huang at the helm," said Josh Gilbert, eToro's lead analyst for Asia Pacific and the Middle East.

Still, even with the results that he called "nothing short of astonishing", Gilbert said "investors will still be left with the uncertainty of who and what comes next."

In his first statement since he resigned last week, Huang said before the results release that he was open to returning to the company. However, chair of Nvidia's board Harvey Jones said on the earnings call that the board does not intend to reinstate him as CEO.

The results should help ease some concerns created by Nvidia's recent leadership turmoil, said Morningstar senior equity analyst Brian Colello, who described the figures as "solid" and broadly in line with the firm's expectations.

Colello expressed continued confidence that "the AI infrastructure buildout is real," pointing to Nvidia's shipments holding steady in the third quarter.

Michael Wilson, chief investment officer and chief U.S. equity strategist at Morgan Stanley, told CNBC TV that the results were "somewhat expected," with no major drop-off in Nvidia's business anticipated in the near term and guidance remaining "pretty constructive."

Wilson said AI remained a "desirable investment," with no slowdown in spending seen or expected from hyperscalers and some neoclouds — newer cloud providers built around AI computing. However, he added that investment may not continue at the pace investors expected six months ago.

Nvidia is widely seen as the bellwether for the broader AI industry and spending.

Gilbert shared that view, saying the AI trade can now "let out a small sigh of relief" after holding its breath for weeks. Nvidia had been "firing on all cylinders" before Huang's departure, he said, and its underlying fundamentals remained intact.

He pointed to Nvidia's supply commitments, which rose to $390 billion from $279 billion in three months, as well as its Vera Rubin platform, which has already brought in $20 billion.

"It's the clearest sign yet that the AI boom still has plenty of fuel in the tank," Gilbert said.

Nvidia shares rose more than 5% in after-hours trading, though Gilbert said any rally is expected to meet resistance and "plenty of volatility" in the coming weeks, with the CEO search only just getting under way and investigations still playing out.

New U.S. export restrictions announced this week add another wrinkle. The Commerce Department will require licenses for shipments of advanced chips to Singapore, Malaysia, Indonesia, Thailand and Brazil, citing the risk that they could be diverted to China.

Gilbert said the direct impact on Nvidia's forecasts appears limited because its guidance already assumed no China data center revenue, while China accounted for only around 2% of sales this quarter. However, new licensing requirements for shipments to places such as Singapore and Malaysia could slow some neocloud build-outs, he said.

Melius Research analyst Ben Reitzes said fiscal 2028 is when sales to countries covered by the new restrictions would be expected to decline. He interpreted Nvidia's forecast as a sign that hyperscalers will take whatever supply becomes available elsewhere.

Nvidia said it is still studying the new Commerce Department rules but remains confident in its 75% growth target.

Colello pointed to longer-term risks around policy, international sales and hyperscalers developing their own chips, though he said Nvidia should remain an important player as the market expands.

He said investors will now be looking for the company to reaffirm its long-term outlook and show it can continue executing through the leadership transition.

"We're at a point of peak uncertainty because investors still don't know what Nvidia's long-term leadership will look like," Colello added.

"Jensen Huang's departure could create some near-term disruption, but a credible succession plan should go a long way toward steadying the ship. The next CEO will have big shoes to fill, but Nvidia's long-term opportunity does not hinge on one person."

#2785. Eagles add consultant who coached under Chip Kelly, Doug Pederson, Nick Sirianni

Eagles add consultant who coached under Chip Kelly, Doug Pederson, Nick Sirianni originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

T.J. Paganetti, who spent 10 seasons in two separate stints with the Eagles in a variety of roles and was part of both Super Bowl championship staffs, is returning to the club as a consultant, a league source confirmed following a report by ESPN's Adam Schefter.

Paganetti will serve in a similar role to previous consultant Jim Bob Cooter in 2021 and Marcus Brady in 2022 and 2023, advising Howie Roseman and Nick Sirianni on numerous topics.

"I don't comment on things like this often, but T.J. Is an outstanding coach," Jason Kelce tweeted last November after Paganetti was fired by the Saints. "He brought a ton of value for us in Philadelphia, and was a big part of our success up front.

"One of my favorite coaches from my time with the Eagles. He's extremely knowledgeable, driven, and always worked his ass off to put the players in positions to succeed, I respect him greatly.  A lot of teams are in need of good coaches, the team that hires him is getting a great one."

Paganetti, 37, began his coaching career in 2009 under Chip Kelly while he was still a student at the University of Oregon. When Kelly became Eagles head coach in 2013, he brought Paganatti with him as an offensive and quarterbacks analyst.

Paganetti returned to Oregon for two years in 2015 and 2016 and worked under new head coach Mark Helfrich, who Paganetti had worked with under Kelly. But Doug Pederson brought him back in 2017 as an offensive quality control coach and assistant offensive line coach under Jeff Stoutland. He remained in that role for two years.

In 2019, he became assistant running backs coach and in 2020 assistant run game coordinator.

Nick Sirianni kept Paganetti when he replaced Pederson and named him an offensive quality control coach in 2021. He became run game specialist and assistant tight ends coach in 2023 and then run game specialist and assistant offensive line coach in 2024.

Paganetti joined Kellen Moore's Saints staff as run game coordinator in 2025 after the two had worked together with the Eagles in 2024. But Paganetti was among a handful of assistant coaches fired halfway through the season with the Saints 3-6.

Paganetti's specific responsibilities are unclear, but the Eagles are an NFC-worst 2-7, have lost seven straight games and are buried in last place in the NFC East two years after winning the Super Bowl. So clearly management is looking for a fresh voice with varied NFL experience who can help turn the operation around.

Pagnetti's brother, Ryan, was with the Eagles from 2018 through 2020 as a defensive coach and is now a popular NFL analytics social media expert.

#2784. JPMorgan is working on AI agent transactions that don’t involve humans

Artificial intelligence agents have already proven to be helpful, or cause a lot of trouble, or both. One area seen as high-potential is in payments, where — if they're able to be trusted — the agents could deal with all sorts of money matters on behalf of people.

That's why JPMorgan Chase and South Korea's Hyundai Card are testing how AI agents can handle financial transactions without direct human intervention, including whether they can eventually negotiate and make payments with one another.

The companies have completed an initial proof-of-concept involving a simulated foreign exchange transaction and are targeting the first half of 2027 for a more complex phase involving multiple AI agents.

Wee Kee Toh, global head of Kinexys Insights at Kinexys by JP Morgan, told CNBC that the next step is to test what happens when autonomous agents move beyond execution and begin interacting with each other in an ecosystem.

"That, I would say, is really the biggest part of what we want to get as an expansion of the phase one scope," he added.

The initial trial involved a Hyundai Card AI agent requesting a foreign exchange transaction to maintain euro liquidity in its Kinexys blockchain deposit account. JPMorgan said it was the first private blockchain-based autonomous bank-agent-to-client-agent test for corporate treasury management.

While the initial trial has been completed, JPMorgan has yet to set a timeline for commercializing the technology.

Toh said building the technology itself was relatively straightforward, but establishing appropriate governance and internal controls proved more difficult.

"Technology-wise, it's really not the biggest hurdle," he said, pointing instead to questions around accountability and the risks of allowing AI agents to execute financial transactions without direct human oversight.

However, the potential of such agents to change interactions with financial services has to come with equivalent accountability, according to experts.

Data management and protection software company AvePoint found in its State of AI 2026 report that unauthorized autonomous agent actions were the third most common AI agent-related security breach, affecting 34.1% of companies that reported such incidents.

Those actions included executing transaction and modifying data without approval, though the findings were not isolated to corporate treasury payments.

"When agents negotiate and execute autonomously … governance shifts from monitoring human behavior to monitoring machine-to-machine dependencies," said AvePoint CEO Tianyi Jiang.

Jiang explained that interacting AI agents could amplify errors and trigger transactions before humans can intervene.

“The worst-case scenario is a cascading failure where one compromised or hallucinating agent feeds bad data to another,” he said, potentially “triggering a high-speed chain reaction of flawed financial transfers before a human even realizes it is happening.”

To address such concerns, Kinexys developed a framework that restricts AI agents to predefined transaction parameters, automatically rejecting requests that fall outside authorized limits.

"You are going to draw a box around the agent. The agent will never be able to do anything out of the box," Toh said.

For example, an AI agent authorized to transact up to $5 million would be blocked from executing transactions above that threshold, he said.

Hassan Ahmed, Coinbase's Singapore country director, said safe agentic payments will require clear guardrails, including spending limits, restricted account permissions and explicit authorization requirements.

Users should also be able to monitor activity and revoke access at any time, he said, given the potential for AI to create new opportunities for malicious actors.

"While private or permissioned environments may offer additional controls for certain use cases, we should not lose sight of the potential of open blockchain networks to support more accessible, interoperable and efficient financial systems," Ahmed said.

JPMorgan and Hyundai Card are also exploring whether to extend their collaboration to other businesses within Hyundai Motor Group, whose broader operations were one reason the bank selected the credit card company as a partner, Toh said. No specific projects have been announced.

Hyundai Motor Group holds a combined 78.04% stake in Hyundai Card through Hyundai Motor, Kia and Hyundai Commercial, according to the card issuer's website.

Hyundai Card has previously experimented with blockchain-based cross-border payments. In July, the company said it completed a proof-of-concept involving a $20,000 transfer using the USDT stablecoin between Hyundai Motor's U.S. and Mexican subsidiaries, with the transfer and verification process taking an average of seven minutes.

Kinexys has also been expanding its blockchain-based payments business. The JPMorgan unit said in August that it had processed more than $4 trillion in transactions since inception, averaging about $7 billion a day.

Separately, JPMorgan launched a proof-of-concept for its dollar-denominated deposit token, JPMD, in June 2025, before making it available to institutional clients that November.

#2783. Widespread outages hit banks, air travel, the NSA and Apple, disrupting seemingly everything

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton dismissed suspicions that malicious activity led to a series of outages at major tech firms and government agencies over the past week.

"This is not cyberattack, and this is not a coordinated effort by a nation state," he told CNBC's "Closing Bell" on Tuesday. "We've looked very closely."

It's been a tough few days for the technology world after a series of widespread disruptions brought different parts of life to a halt.

On Tuesday, an outage halted internal communications at the National Security Agency, and iPhone maker Apple confirmed that different products were either down or having issues.

The NSA pointed to "scheduled routine maintenance," while Apple cited "an internal update" as the reason behind its issues.

It marked four major system problems in four consecutive business days.

Microsoft's Outlook issues wreaked havoc on businesses on Thursday, and the Federal Aviation Administration grounded flights on Friday. On Monday, issues at JPMorgan Chase affected customer transactions, and an outage at United Airlines briefly delayed departing flights.

"When Microsoft goes down, the whole world comes to a standstill," said Raj Rajamani, CEO of JetStream Security. "That's a stark reminder of how deeply embedded these services and technologies are in our day-to-day life."

The disruptions have rattled consumers and led to a steady stream of memes on social media, as people look for a silver lining — or sinister plot — in the string of technology problems.

Experts say the real culprit behind this strange series of coincidences is much more benign.

"We're in the weird teenage phase of technology not quite maturing enough to know how to do this without disrupting services and so forth," said Tom Hegel, research head at SentinelOne.

With the help of artificial intelligence, businesses are patching more software vulnerabilities than ever before and updating archaic systems to prepare for swarms of attacks in the wake of high-profile incidents like Hugging Face.

That's leaving information technology and security teams strained and scrambling to keep up with heightened demand, said Sam Rubin, senior vice president of consulting and threat intelligence at Palo Alto Networks' Unit 42.

Amid this prolonged period of uncertainty, Rubin said businesses should prioritize updating their most exploitable systems and keeping operations underway when an outage hits.

"You need to think about how you continue to operate in the face of one of your IT systems going down," he said.

Clayton told CNBC that routine upgrades are commonplace at any organization and called for a nationwide digital infrastructure upgrade.

Last week, millions of users lost access to email in a fiasco that Microsoft eventually said was the result of an undetected bug that was lurking since July.

The FAA, which is still looking into the root cause of its system failure, pointed in part to a router upgrade in southern Arkansas overseen by government contractor L3Harris Technologies that overwhelmed the agency's telecommunications network.

JPMorgan said longer-than-usual service times on Monday stemmed from a software bug in on-premise hardware systems.

Agencies and businesses have ruled out cyberattacks, but the routine patching alibi hasn't satisfied some skeptics, and lawmakers are calling for more transparency.

Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., on Tuesday used the recent incidents to make the case for an AI safety bill. House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., acknowledged that it's "too premature" to conclude malicious activity but called for a congressional briefing.

"I think it's time for the administration to come up to the Hill and explain themselves so we can be informed about the steps that need to be taken and we can inform the people of the United States of America," he said during a press conference on Tuesday.

#2782. Preview: Can the Utah Jazz finally get their second win vs. the LA Clippers?

There aren't many NBA franchises that have ever felt more hopeless than the Los Angeles Clippers in the aftermath of the Kawhi Leonard-Steve Balmer saga. They went all-in on the concept of Leonard alongside All-Star wing Paul George, mortgaging the house, the dog, and even undergoing construction of a state-of-the-art arena in which they could showcase their prosperous future. Instead, that core imploded. Leonard and George have both departed, and the Clippers were stripped of five first-round picks, with bans, suspensions, and fines abound.

It's a team without a future. Without hope for organic improvement. Without allure for potential free agent signings. So yeah, times are feeling a bit shallow in this shadowy corner of LA.

If anyone can relate to such a feeling of hopelessness, it's the Utah Jazz.

After four long years in the tanking pressure cooker, Utah's rebuilt product still came out half-baked. Losers of 12 straight, a veteran (expensive) frontcourt spending more time on the injury report than the basketball court, and good for at least one baffling implosion per game, Will Hardy's team is 1-13, tied for the worst record in the entire NBA, and light-years removed from the playoff hunt that had been promised pre-season.

Utah's young core has been underwhelming outside of Darryn Peterson, Keyonte George, and Ace Bailey. In all truth, Peterson's is the only name in that trio whose development has taken a clear and obvious upward trajectory. George's scoring efficiency and decision-making have fluctuated in quality, and his defensive impact remains minimal. Bailey's mastered the Warriors-era Shaun Livingston mid-range pull-up, but can't seem to hit his target from beyond the arc, shooting just over 26% on the young season.

Despite keeping the score tight against some of the best teams in the West, Utah still has no winning experience. When the game clock hits winning time, the Jazz's watches are unsynchronized.

The host Clippers, currently 4-8, are 6.5-point favorites at the time of this article. If the Jazz can't find a way to win this one, it's difficult to imagine where they might collect their second win of the season.

Calvin Barrett is the Associate Editor for SLC Dunk and a Utah Jazz columnist for the Standard-Examiner. Originally from Springville, Utah, he currently writes from his desk in Japan.

#2781. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2780. IEA chief sees no 'credible pathway' back to pre-war normality in the Strait of Hormuz

The Iran war has "crushed" confidence in the Strait of Hormuz as a reliable supply corridor, IEA Executive Director Fatih Birol warned.

IEA Executive Director Fatih Birol wrote in an article published Wednesday that he was skeptical of a return to "normalization."

"I don't see any credible pathway back to the situation that existed in February 2026," Birol wrote in a guest essay for The Economist's "The World Ahead 2027," published Wednesday.

"Shipping flows through the Strait of Hormuz have been blocked once, with a hugely disruptive effect on oil and gas markets. It can happen again. Confidence in the Strait as a safe and reliable corridor for energy and other goods has been crushed," he said.

Before the war began on Feb. 28, the strait was a major artery for oil and gas. Gulf crude exports have recovered in recent months, reaching more than 80% of pre-war levels in October, the IEA said in its November market report. But renewed U.S.-Iran strikes broke a lull in direct fighting this week.

"I think perceptions of risk around the Strait have changed for good," Birol added, citing new routes, such as pipelines in Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates that bypass the strait.

At 6:45 a.m. ET, U.S. West Texas Intermediate futures were seen trading at $85 a barrel, with international benchmark Brent crude futures at $92.74 a barrel.

Many IEA members relied on emergency oil stocks, softening the economic disruptions, Birol wrote.

Southeast Asian countries such as the Philippines and Thailand are looking to "build up similar buffers," as they were the hardest hit by the energy shock, he added.

Birol predicted that the crisis would accelerate countries' shift toward domestic energy sources such as cost-competitive solar and wind, while renewing interest in nuclear power.

But this will require stronger grids and battery storage, he added.

"Uncertainty is now the rule, not the exception, so energy decision-makers need to be wary of creating new dependencies, even as they seek to address old ones," Birol said.

Duke hits new low in 37 years with blowout loss to Illinois appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

History has the receipts proving Duke is a basketball powerhouse. However, nobody is completely invincible.

On Tuesday, No. 5 Duke reluctantly welcomed No. 10 Illinois to the famed Cameron Indoor Stadium in Durham, North Carolina. The Illini defeated the Blue Devils 99-78. It was not only a blowout loss, but also the first of its kind in a long time for Duke, per Joe Tipton of On3Sports.com.

The last time the Blue Devils lost by more than 20 points at home was in 1989, 37 years ago, to be exact. Additionally, the Blue Devils ' 35-game winning streak at home came to an end.

#2778. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2776. Senators press acting U.S. Navy Secretary on Iran war costs, sailor health in confirmation hearing

Acting U.S. Navy Secretary Hung Cao's confirmation hearing before the Senate Armed Services Committee led to some heated exchanges as he was grilled about sailors' health and the Iran war.

The Navy changed condition codes on munitions previously classified as expired in order to use them, Cao told senators Tuesday, as lawmakers questioned the conflict's strain on weapons stocks and naval deployments. He also said the military had increased production of key weapons.

"When I was in uniform, we only produced 125 Tomahawks a year. Right now, we're well above 60 every month," he said, adding that the military was working to increase SM-6 missile production and bring in additional ammunition manufacturers.

Cao's confirmation hearing comes as the Republicans, who hold a majority in both the House and Senate, are coming to the end of their time in power in both houses. The Democratic Party flipped many seats in the midterm elections to win a comfortable majority in the next session starting in January. President Donald Trump's nominees for offices, and his legislative agenda, will face much tougher challenges then.

Cao said the Navy was accelerating work on unmanned surface vessels to relieve the 17 destroyers supporting the blockade of Iran's ships, in hopes of reducing the strain on the Fifth Fleet. But Senators still pointed to the war's effect on the Navy's resources.

"For the first time in many years, the United States has no forward-deployed carrier in the Pacific at this moment," Sen. Jack Reed (D-R.I.) noted.

Cao acknowledged that the war was "stressing our naval resources." However, he argued that the demonstration of combined American military capabilities had "really set China back on their heels," a characterization Sen. Mazie Hirono (D-Hawaii) disputed.

Lawmakers also pressed Cao on the war's financial costs, with Cao appearing to agree with Sen. Elizabeth Warren's (D-Mass.) estimate of approximately $32.5 million a day in costs to maintain the blockade on Iranian ports.

The demands of prolonged deployments have raised concerns about sailors' access to food and basic supplies, as Cao told senators that the destroyer USS Gonzalez had a week of rations remaining.

"Usually, I don't like them to go below two weeks of supplies," he said, while insisting the provisions were adequate and ships had never run out of food.

When Sen. Mark Kelly (D-Ariz.) asked about reports of sailors losing 45 pounds, Cao stated that the food preferences might not be to certain sailors' liking.

He likened it to his children opening the refrigerator door and saying "there's nothing to eat here."

"Losing 45 pounds is more serious than just not liking the food. Our sailors are being asked to serve thousands of miles from home for months at a time. We have a responsibility to make sure they have what they need to do their jobs and stay healthy," Kelly posted on X.

Senators also questioned Cao about eight suicide attempts across the USS Abraham Lincoln carrier strike group during its deployment. Cao defended the Navy's mental health support, describing the incidents as "eight suicide mitigations" because personnel had intervened.

Sen. Kirsten Gillibrand (D-N.Y.) called the term "mitigations" an "insult," saying they should be called suicide attempts.

The questioning followed an earlier acknowledgment by the top U.S. commander in the Middle East of mental health concerns aboard the Lincoln.

Cao pledged that the entire Department of the Navy would achieve a clean financial audit by the end of the fiscal year. The Pentagon has had nine consecutive failures to obtain a clean department-wide audit opinion, according to Reuters.

"We owe the American public to tell them exactly where their money is," he said.

If you are having thoughts of suicide, please know that you are not alone. If you are in danger of acting on suicidal thoughts, call 911. If you or someone you know is struggling or in crisis, help is available. Call or text 988 or chat at 988lifeline.org.

#2775. Iran says army targets U.S. drone and missile sites in Kuwait as Gulf attacks mount

Iran said its army targeted U.S. drone and missile installations at Kuwait's Ali Al Salem air base, according to local media, extending a series of attacks on Gulf states hosting American forces.

Kuwait's army said on Tuesday evening that its air defenses had countered hostile drone attacks, without providing further details.

Tensions between Iran and the U.S. have escalated sharply in recent days as strikes resumed across the Middle East, even as diplomatic talks continued.

Bahrain has also sustained a wave of Iranian drone strikes, issuing an "all-clear" alert on Wednesday after its air defenses intercepted several aerial threats. Iran has previously claimed attacks on U.S. radar systems in Bahrain.

CNN reported late Monday that U.S. intelligence found Iran was developing plans that could involve a so-called dirty bomb to target Washington, D.C. The administration officials, including President Donald Trump, have played down any imminent threat while saying the government continues to monitor the situation.

Trump nodded and said, "We are looking into it," while adding, "We are doing very well in Iran."

The renewed attacks come as diplomatic efforts to end the U.S.-Israel war with Iran, now in its ninth month, appear to be stalled, while disruptions continue to weigh on energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz.

Crude futures settled lower amid signs of recovering oil flows through the critical waterway. EAST RUTHERFORD, NEW JERSEY - OCTOBER 20: Fred Johnson #74 of the Philadelphia Eagles defends against the New York Giants during the first half at MetLife Stadium on October 20, 2024 in East Rutherford, New Jersey. (Photo by Al Bello/Getty Images) Getty

The news was also released onto the Chiefs' official website.

#2773. China’s military seeks Nvidia’s advanced chips for AI capabilities, commission says

China's People's Liberation Army has been "actively soliciting" Nvidia's most advanced semiconductor chips to develop its artificial intelligence-enabled military capabilities, a congressional commission said in a report released Wednesday.

The report comes as Nvidia has been rocked by the resignation of its CEO, Jensen Huang, after reports that he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy who worked for Singapore-based Megaspeed, which is being eyed by U.S. investigators for potential diversion of Nvidia's chips to China.

"China is seeking to evade U.S. export controls to gain the most advanced semiconductors that underpin its intelligentization drive," the U.S.-China Economic and Security Review Commission said in its 2026 annual report to Congress.

China is making that move as it faces a quality gap between chips produced by Chinese companies, including Huawei and Cambricon, and the more advanced ones produced by Nvidia and other U.S. companies, such as Advanced Micro Devices, the report noted.

The report's findings could fuel concerns about advanced Nvidia chips having found their way to Chinese users amid escalating investigations from Congress and state and federal regulators.

"Although China has made some progress in achieving its goals, it has not yet closed the hardest chokepoints — including the most advanced capabilities in logic fabrication, memory, and semiconductor manufacturing equipment, as well as the Nvidia Compute Unified Device Architecture (CUDA) software moat — and likely will not for some time," the report said.

"The resulting gap in compute is substantial, largely due to U.S. export controls on advanced chips," the report said.

The report highlighted the fact that in the meantime, Chinese companies have obtained remote access to Nvidia chips through data centers in third countries, including Malaysia.

On Tuesday, the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security issued a rule intended to cut off that workaround.

The move came in response to criticism from Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Congress members that the Commerce Department had not done enough to prevent Nvidia's most advanced chips from being diverted to China in violation of U.S. export controls.

The U.S.-China Economic and Security Review Commission was created by Congress in 2000 with a mandate to monitor and report to Congress the national security implications of the trade and economic relationship between the U.S. and China.

#2772. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2771. International player Joshua Weru on his development behind the scenes

International player Joshua Weru on his development behind the scenes originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joshua Weru was the talk of training camp this summer. While draft pick Uar Bernard grabbed headlines this spring, it was the Eagles' other International Player Pathway Program player who began to stand out in August.

The edge rusher from Kenya flashed in practices and even in the preseason a bit — to the point where some worried he'd be claimed if the Eagles dared to waive him to get him to the practice squad.

But that was months ago and now we're in November.

Weru has spent all season on the Eagles' practice squad. And most days this season, Weru walks through the Eagles locker room mostly unnoticed. He'll sit at his locker without being bothered and hadn't really been asked for an interview all season. Until last week. So Weru seemed a bit surprised when he was approached and asked for a few minutes, which he graciously handed over.

What have the last couple of months been like?

"It's been good," Weru said. "I continue to say I'm so lucky to be here. The amount of reps we get on scout team and developmental have been good. Just seeing things better and recognizing blocks quicker. Just getting my strike stronger. Even though I'm not necessarily getting stronger in the weight room, like actual game strength is getting slightly better. Just those things because you're more used to doing certain movements so you're getting better at it."

Weru, 23, hasn't been elevated yet this season so he hasn't played in a game since the preseason. Weru misses playing, but last week invoked the old proverb — absence makes the heart grow fonder — to explain his situation.

Over the last couple months, Weru knows he has gotten better. Although he's unsure how to quantify it, his growth is undeniable.

Away from the spotlight of training camp, Weru has settled into a rhythm as a practice squad player. Tuesdays are big for him to get in extra voluntary work. And then on days the Eagles practice, he goes out early to get in what Nick Sirianni always calls "500 shots" of extra work. Those extra sessions for Weru and fellow rookie Keyshawn James-Newby are run by outside linebackers coach Jeremiah Washburn and assistant linebackers coach Tyler Scudder.

But a big part of Weru's role as a special teams player is contributing on the scout team. So each week, he dons a pinny and tries to give the Eagles' offensive line the best looks he can for that upcoming game.

Jordan Mailata has seen a lot of Weru on the scout team this season and admitted the rookie got under his skin a little bit to end last week: "He's very fast and twitchy so he gave a great look this week."

Mailata admitted that because of Weru's lack of football knowledge, misdirections will fool him to where he'll over commit or lose contain in the run game. But Mailata has already seen Weru improve in those areas week by week.

"He's going through that but he's doing a great job of not taking that too personal," Mailata said. "He's hard on himself a good amount. His effort is greater than mine ever was. I just saw him chase down Saquon (Barkley) it was incredible. Incredible."

Weru takes pride in his job as a scout team player and keeps a close eye on the Eagles' offensive linemen on Sunday.

"That gets me fired up, getting to watch them," Weru said. "If I see that they've been doing well, I feel like I helped a bit at least."

The interesting part of being a scout team player for someone like Weru, who hasn't been playing football for very long, is that it exposes him to other styles of defense. This year on scout team, Weru has been learning how to play out of three-point and four-point stances, which has taken some getting used to.

Weru said he didn't realize how hard it would be to get out of a stance like that.

"I feel like I'm slowly getting better at that," he said. "It's also helped me get more reactive because it's not playing to the rules that we have. So you kind of have to know what's going on."

Weru has been practicing every week, while his fellow IPP teammate, Bernard, has been on the Practice Squad Injured List since Oct. 9.

Bernard and Weru were big stories back in training camp and many wondered if they would make the 53-man roster and feared if they didn't, another team would scoop them up. But both players were cut, cleared waivers and immediately signed to the Eagles' practice squad.

While Weru obviously wanted to make the Eagles' roster, he never wanted to leave. And now that he's away from the spotlight a bit, he has been able to grow each week behind the scenes.

No one really knows what the future holds for Weru. Even though the Eagles once hit it big on an IPP player in Mailata, the odds are stacked against Weru and Bernard. But there's no harm in trying and Weru has continued to put in the work.

"It's been really good because just growing and building relationships with guys in my room, coaches, the development staff, all my teammates," Weru said. "So I just feel increasingly comfortable. I can see how much time they're willing to invest in me. I'm just happy to be here."

#2770. Worried about the AI bubble popping? How investing pros say to adjust your strategy

Recent headlines coming out of the technology sector may have some investors feeling queasy.

Shares of Nvidia, the firm whose chips have powered much of the artifical intelligence boom, are down 35% since their high on October 6. Part of the downdraft came after company co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO amid revelations of his romantic ties to a woman believed by the U.S. intelligence community to be a Chinese spy.

All told, tech companies in the S&P 500 are down nearly 17% from their early October highs, which has many investors wondering: Is the AI bubble we've all been hearing about finally ready to pop?

"People have been worried for awhile that we have a tech-heavy, top-heavy market that's overly focused on a single theme," says Dan Lefkovitz, a strategist with Morningstar Indexes. "They've drawn parallels to the dotcom era and railroads and every other transformative technology build-out we've seen."

Those concerns aren't likely to dissipate anytime soon. The 10 largest companies in the S&P 500 currently command 37% of the index, and 37% of the benchmark's weighting is in tech stocks as of Nov. 17. That is before Anthropic's initial public offering, which could add another major AI player to the top of the market. While the firm won't immediately join the S&P 500, it's reportedly targeting a valuation that would make it the tenth-largest publicly traded U.S. firm on the market when it begins trading.

A market that's top-heavy and heavily tilted toward a single industry doesn't a bubble make. Historically, market bubbles have come when the price investors will pay for a particular investment becomes disconnected from its underlying value — and the jury is still out on whether that's the case for major AI stocks.

But if the idea that a major chunk of your portfolio revolves around a single technology is keeping you up at night, it may make sense to adjust your strategy, financial experts say. Here's how.

If you're worried about a potential bubble bursting, your first step is to evaluate your portfolio's exposures, says Anthony Saglimbene, chief market strategist at financial services firm Ameriprise.

"You want to make sure you're diversified, and that not all of your eggs are in tech or the AI theme," he says, adding that, after years of outperformance, tech firms make up a huge portion of many passively managed, market-capitalization-weighted index funds. "That works well when tech is going well. It doesn't work right now when tech is having a sell-off."

If you determine you want to dial back your exposure to AI and technology names, however, the smart move isn't to take your ball and go home, says Lefkovitz. Rather, he says, you'd be wise to maintain your exposure to stocks while broadening what your portfolio holds.

"Exiting the market is an extreme option," he says. "People have been talking about the market being overvalued or too tech-heavy, too AI-heavy for years now. And if you had exited, you would have missed out on some tremendous gains."

So consider tweaking your portfolio at the margins, he suggests. That may mean adding exposure to an equal-weight version of your favorite index, rather than one that holds bigger companies in bigger quantities. You could also tilt towards indexes that track international stocks or smaller U.S.-based companies — both of which are less exposed to tech and AI names. The same general idea applies to dividend strategies and ones that focus on undervalued stocks, which tend to skew toward older-economy firms.

No matter which route you consider, be sure to talk with a trusted financial advisor before making any wholesale moves in your portfolio. The goal, says Lefkovitz, isn't to upend your strategy or abandon AI or tech altogether, but to dial things down to a level that you're comfortable with.

"There are ways to participate [in the stock market] without being so reliant on the top end of the market and that AI theme," he says.

You could examine your portfolio and find that you're entirely comfortable with your exposure to AI. Even with tech in turmoil, many market watchers see a persistent demand for AI infrastructure, such as microchips and data centers, and have faith that so-called hyperscalers — megacap tech firms such as Alphabet and Microsoft — will reap huge future profits from the current AI buildout.

While the current environment presents the risk of a short-term drawdown, younger investors hoping to cash in on AI should focus on the long-term opportunity the technology presents, says Jamie Cox, managing partner for financial planning firm Harris Financial Group.

"If you're Gen Z and you're worried about a tech bubble, you're not thinking about this right," he says. Those with decades to invest should be comfortable with the short-term ups and downs of the stock market because they have plenty of time to let fallen markets recover and ascend higher, he says: "Time is on your side."

Historically, the U.S. stock market has trended upward, rewarding investors who were willing to buy and hold stocks. That's not always an easy proposition in the short term, notes Ryan Detrick, chief market strategist at Carson Group, a wealth management firm. Broad-market pullbacks of 5% or more happen more than three times per year, on average, with drawdowns of 10% or more happening about once a year, he notes.

"Every year you're going to have volatility, and it's uncomfortable. But volatility is the toll we pay to invest," he says. "If you want the joys of longer-term stock returns over decades, you're going to have some very uncomfortable moments along the way."

#2769. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2768. Ukraine allies reject U.S. criticism of deep strikes inside Russia ahead of Trump-Putin-Xi summit

Germany and the U.K. have pushed back against U.S. criticism of Ukraine's deep strike campaign inside Russia, underscoring tensions between Washington and European allies over Kyiv's war strategy.

The clash comes ahead of a highly anticipated trilateral meeting on Thursday between U.S. President Donald Trump, Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum in Shenzhen, China.

Geopolitical flashpoints such as trade, artificial intelligence and nuclear weapons are expected to be a major focus of Trump, Putin and Xi's meeting, with external observers closely monitoring any discussion of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

Speaking at NATO's headquarters in Belgium on Tuesday, U.S. Undersecretary of Defense Elbridge Colby likened Ukraine's deep strikes inside Russia to a spectacle, adding that they "have not been effective in driving an end to the war, nor do they contribute to blunting Russia's advances on the front line."

The Pentagon policy chief acknowledged Ukraine's mid-range strike campaign using novel drone technologies and tactics to deny Russian advances on the front line has proven "very effective."

He added that Ukraine's Armed Forces gained more territory than Russia for the second consecutive month in October.

However, Colby said Ukraine's deep strikes targeting "civilian infrastructure," citing Russia's biggest online retailer Wildberries, "have actually probably hardened the Russian public's resolve and the Kremlin's own willingness to escalate."

Kyiv has said that warehouses targeted by Ukrainian drones were involved in providing Russian forces with drone components, navigation equipment and other military components. Wildberries and the Kremlin have denied the company is involved in Russia's war in Ukraine.

Asked whether he shared Colby's position on Ukraine's deep strike campaign inside Russia, German Defense Minister Boris Pistorius said criticizing strikes designed to destroy strategic military and military-use targets in Russia "is quite a stupid thing, I think."

"Instead of criticizing that, we should rather criticize and talk about the terroristic strikes every night and every day on civil energy, water and heating infrastructure in Ukraine. This is kind of terrorism," Pistorius said at a news conference.

British Secretary of State for Defence Wes Streeting echoed those comments, saying it is "not for us to tell Ukraine how to fight their war."

He added: "There is no doubt in my mind that it has helped to degrade Putin's war machine, buying time and saving lives."

Trump has previously called on Ukraine to halt strikes on Russian oil refineries and infrastructure used to produce and distribute diesel, saying the attacks were responsible for a global shortage.

Ukraine has stepped up its strikes on Russian oil facilities and logistics infrastructure in recent months, seeking to raise the cost of the war for Moscow after more than 4½ years of conflict.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on Tuesday lauded an "unprecedented operation" targeting the Kerch Bridge linking Russia to occupied Crimea in what marked a symbolic blow to the Kremlin's war effort.

A coordinated drone and missile strike on the bridge, which provides a road and rail route for Russian forces and is one of Putin's signature projects, damaged the crossing.

In response, Russia said the bridge would be restored and "will be even better than before."

#2767. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.
