#2719. Awkward Jim Harbaugh-Justin Herbert Moment Adds Weird Vibe To Chargers Win

It took nine games, but the Los Angeles Chargers got their first win of the 2026 regular season on Monday night against the Baltimore Ravens. A 1-8 record is not much to celebrate, and an awkward moment in the locker room after the game between head coach Jim Harbaugh and quarterback Justin Herbert just gave the whole thing a very weird vibe.

In a video shared on social media which can be viewed here, Harbaugh congratulated the team for the win and then implored everyone to give each other a high five. The camera catches Harbaugh and Herbert exchanging a high five where the signal caller did not look all that enthused as he lightly exchanged a high five with his head coach.

Some on social media saw the moment as evidence that Herbert doesn't like Harbaugh or that he doesn't have a good attitude. Others defended Herbert, asking whether he should have been doing back flips for their first win of the season. It's probably just a moment that got blown way out of proportion but when a team has had as terrible a season as the Chargers, every single little moment is going to be scrutinized whether it is fair or not.

To give Herbert the benefit of the doubt, it has been a brutal season. He probably doesn't want to be celebrating too hard when his team is just having a completely embarrassing year. Plus, Herbert has always been fairly mild-mannered and reserved. Mina Kimes wrote a whole profile about Herbert years ago for ESPN which delved into how he is an introvert and just simply isn't the big personality that many people expect an NFL quarterback to be.

That is what makes the Herbert-Harbaugh pairing so interesting on paper. Harbaugh is loud, bombastic, and prone to outbursts. Herbert is serene, cool, and supremely understated. It wouldn't seem like the two would work together well but sometimes opposites attract in relationships.

The last two seasons the two seemed to work together quite well as the team went 11-6 and made the playoffs each season. Maybe the addition of offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel is what threw things off a bit but Herbert has remained steadfast in his support of his offensive playcaller.

Maybe the moment in the locker room is evidence of Herbert showing his displeasure with his head coach, or maybe it's just how Herbert is. He and Harbaugh are completely different personality-wise so most likely it is just a case of two very different people sharing a bit of an awkward moment which wouldn't even be blinked at if the team was 8-1 rather than 1-8.

#2718. Scouts: Nets biggest problem is roster construction

Things are bad in Brooklyn. The team isn't just 3-10, tied for the second worst in the Eastern Conference and fans are up in arms, justifiably. After two years of tanking and a lot of hope, the Nets aren't just losing. They look disorganized even unprepared. The head coach and general manager are facing the brunt of fans' frustration with calls for both to be fired. That, at the moment, seems unlikely.

So, Brian Lewis spoke to a number of NBA scouts. What, he asked, is the problem? The answer for almost all of them was poor roster construction which, of course, is Marks's responsibility. Of course, as (most) people inside will tell you, this was always supposed to be a development season and working out rotations should be expected. Still, tell that to fans.

"The lineups don't really fit," one Eastern Conference scout told Lewis, writing for New York Post Sports+. "It's like super jumbo right now. Sometimes it's super small, you have like three guards out there. The roster just isn't very clean. … There's small guards or some big guards, but they're guards who need to ball. The lack of shooting on the roster, that can be an issue."

Part of the issue, Lewis writes, is the team's recent spate of injury and illness. It's handcuffed Jordi Fernandez's line-up decisions. As Lewis notes, Monday's game against the 76ers marked just the fourth time Egor Dëmin, Mikel Brown Jr. and Nolan Traoré were all available, and they never had shared the court at the same time.

"It probably eventually gets to … they need to start Brown and Dëmin. So, yeah, eventually that's what it has to get to," a veteran scout said. "The back end is what the back end is. But yeah, it's tough when you have five firsts and it looks like two sure misses and probably three, more than likely three misses."

Indeed, the Nets 2025 draft haul was a frequent refrain. Of the five — picks at Nos. 8, 19, 22, 26 and 27 — there's a lot of complaint about both the selections and use. Scouts agree, as Lewis wrote.

Other than Dëmin and Traoré, the other three have yet to prove themselves as viable NBA rotation pieces. Danny Wolf has shown improvement this season, starting four games as an emergency two-guard. Ben Saraf and Drake Powell are out of the rotation and in the G League.

One scout suggested that Demin, taken at No. 8 in 2025, is not fulfilling the Nets hopes.

"There's not a lot of identity," the scout said. "In terms of guys that I like, I think Traoré actually has gotten better. He was pretty rotten last year. I know he was young, but he couldn't handle [the] physicality of the game. He couldn't handle ball pressure. He couldn't throw it upcourt. But he got better.

"Egor, he had a really good preseason, but it hasn't really translated. His shooting has not really shown that much. You just wonder. It's great to have size, but if you can't break the paint as a lead guard that makes it kind of hard."

The same scout also questioned whether Powell, Saraf and even Wolf are going to work out.

"And the other ones, the other first rounders of that five, you wonder are they NBA players. Saraf? Powell? And then Wolf. They probably should have consolidated that and tried to move up. I don't know [with] who.

Marks & Co. did try to move up, but ultimately wound up settling for quantity. Just last week, the Nets extended the Flatbush Five through 2027=28 so they've not given up on development. As for Brown Jr., the scout likes what he sees so far and isn't bothered by the so-called "babying" of the 20-year-old. Nor, he says, it is that unusual.

"I like Brown. I mean, he's been wild, but he's showing [ability]. He at least has something. There were four guards [drafted] back-to-back-to-back [in 2026], [Keaton] Wagler, him, [Darius] Acuff and [Kingston] Flemings. He's been in-and-out. He's on a minutes limit, which is actually kind of good sometimes to work your way into it. I saw Flemings start the other night, and he played 17 minutes, so that's fine."

As for the team's two veteran bigs, it's become clear that Michael Porter Jr. is the big contributor … and that he won't be around after the February trade deadline.

"It's a tough watch," the Eastern Conference scout said. "Michael is the engine to all of it. He's the hardest guy to guard in terms of actions, ball movement. He's so big, it's hard to switch on him. It's a challenge to guard that dude.

"There's an action where he's like in the paint and [curls], a Spanoulis action. It's a really hard thing to guard, because if they switch a small on him, he's so big, he shoots it over him. And also based on body position, they can throw a lob over the top. Even the SLOB [sideline out-of-bounds] they run, he'll swing. He's just hard to guard because he's so big and his release point is so high. … And he's good at the late air passes to people. And he has good chemistry with [Day'Ron] Sharpe because Sharpe sets good screens, so he opens it up. And then if X5 [the opposing center] shows up, he has a drop down to Sharpe, so you get rewarded."

The plan, it appears, is to use MPJ to not to secure a superstar to make everything well and good, but to garner yet more assets so they can outbid anyone for a big player next year. Will it work? Seems risky but no rebuild is without a lot of it … and at the moment, it's a very mixed bag.

As for the team's only All-Star, Julius Randle, the scout is a bit mystified about how he's being used.

"Julius actually looked good earlier in the year. And they were doing some of the inverted pick-and-roll stuff. Doesn't really seem like he's in a great rhythm right now," the Eastern Conference scout said. "And they're not really running a lot of actions for him. They were running [stuff]. But they were great in the preseason. Which also tells you, yeah, it's the preseason. There's a reason."

That's on the head coach of course. The same scout also takes a lot at the team's atrocious defense, not untypical for such a young team — youngest in the NBA again this season. Still, it can't be ignored.

"How many guys do they have that can actually guard?" one scout asked. Not a lot.

Fernández has largely eschewed playing drop coverage for more aggressive defenses designed to pressure the ball and create turnovers, having the Nets play up at the level of the screen. Fernández has correctly asserted that the best championship contenders play that way, leaning into the modern possession game.

Anyone thinking that Joe Tsai will make a dramatic move before season's end. That's the general impression NBA executives have shared. Marks of course has been GM for 11 years, the third longest in the NBA but there's every indication the owner and his GM are on the same page — the long-term rebuild defined by a strategy build around smart drafting and development. Building organically for a brighter day.

There are however rumors of Nets long-term planning beyond Marks. Not surprising of course but don't expect much in terms of detail for a while.

#2715. Tennessee held Michigan State to a historic nine made shots in win

Tennessee changed the narrative on its early season concerns with a statement win against Michigan State, and it did so with a historic defensive performance.

In the 61-46 Volunteers win, Michigan State made just nine shots, its fewest in a game over 30 years, according to the ESPN broadcast, in what was one of the roughest shooting nights in Tom Izzo's entire tenure. It was also the fewest made shots by any Division I team since January 2024, according to ESPN Insights.

"We got punched in the mouth tonight," Izzo said postgame.

It started off a defensive battle, with Tennessee holding a 27-25 lead at halftime after the Spartans made six shots in the opening frame. But out of halftime, the clamps were on Sparty.

In the second half, Michigan State went 3-for-26 from the field. The Spartans didn't make a shot until five minutes into the half, and then made another nearly four minutes later.

Michigan State then went seven minutes without making a shot, getting its final field goal with four minutes left in the game. In the final 11 minutes, the Spartans 1-for-13 from the field, resulting in the 9-for-50 (18%) night with a 4-for-19 (21.1%) mark from 3-point land, with Izzo calling it an "an all-time low."

That also includes star Jeremy Fears Jr. not making a single shot on the night.

The only reason Michigan State stayed in the game for the majority of the second half was getting to the foul line. In total, Michigan State's 46 points came from the free throw line, with Fears getting all 16 of his points from the charity stripe.

It was an epic and signature defensive performance for Rick Barnes, who is now a perfect 7-0 at home against teams in the top five of the Associated Press poll. With some big performances from Miles Rubin and Dewayne Brown, the Volunteers have gotten back on course to assert themselves as a contender.

#2714. Jose Alvarado making Knicks presence felt — even if he’s not on court

TORONTO — Without playing time, Jose Alvarado has channeled his energy into being the No. 1 Knicks supporter.

The third-string point guard has been, by far, the most boisterous and engaged Knick on the bench despite being pulled from the rotation a couple of weeks ago.

"I think it's more how I was raised [by] my parents," Alvarado said, who scored two points and had four assists in seven minutes in the Knicks' 111-102 win over the Raptors on Wednesday night. "Treat people how you want to be treated. Sometimes the situation isn't going to be what you want. You can't control certain things and control what you can and that's your attitude, your energy every day. So I try to do that.

Jose Alvarado greets forward Mohamed Diawara as he walks back to the bench during the Knicks' blowout win over the Bucks on Nov. 10, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

"I'm human, I have days where it's not going to be all lovey-dovey. But I always tell myself, 'Whether I'm not playing or not, I got the best seats in the house.' And I get to support my teammates. So I try to go with that mindset as best I can and my teammates allow me to be myself."

In a viral moment from Sunday's victory over the Raptors, Alvarado was filmed comforting a visibly dejected Mikal Bridges, who was struggling and on the bench.

Jose Alvarado looks on during the Knicks' win over the Nets on Nov. 2, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. NBAE via Getty Images

Alvarado signed a three-year, $14.4 million contract in the summer to return to the Knicks, but suffered an early ankle sprain and lost his role to the more offensive-minded Kolek.

Alvarado entered Tuesday night against the Raptors with zero appearances longer than 10 minutes since Oct. 23.

"The season is long," Alvarado said. "I've been on the other side. When I first got in the league [in 2021], I wasn't even touching the court. Then eventually I got to touch the court and get some reps in and played a huge role in the rotation.

"Sometimes you get out of it. The only way to get back is stay ready because an opportunity is going to come, you just don't know when. So that's the hardest thing is the guessing game of when that will happen. But you work, work and work and trust that it will come in the end of the day."

#2713. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2712. At NC State, Philip Rivers will be one of the lowest-paid Power 4 coaches

Former NFL quarterback Philip Rivers earned $244 million during his playing career. He won’t be adding to it appreciably during the early years of his coaching career.

Via Kennington Smith III of Front Office Sports, Rivers will make $3.25 million in his first year as the head coach at NC State.

That puts him among the lowest-paid Power 4 coaches whose compensation is publicly known, and in the same range as Cincinnati’s Scott Satterfield ($3.8 million), Oklahoma State’s Eric Morris ($3.8 million), West Virginia’s Rich Rodriguez ($3.6 million), and Iowa State’s Jimmy Rogers ($3 million).

The less Rivers takes, the more that is available for players. And Rivers is confident that the investment will be made.

“It’s been very clear in our talks that they’re committed to doing everything that they can do to help the football program,” Rivers said at his introductory press conference, via Smith. “This thing is still about people. Having belief and confidence in people. So for me, we don’t need to pull out spreadsheets and go through every penny; but it’s a belief that we’re going to do everything we can to get North Carolina State to the top.”

Rivers also realizes that his lack of experience points to a lower entry-level rate, beyond the question of leaving money around for his staff and his players.

“You can’t negate the experience I’ve had in 25 years, but the truth is I am a first-time head coach in Power 4 football,” Rivers said. “So I think in some ways that makes sense in that reflection from a contract standpoint, but those other things are true too.”

If it works, he’ll surely get more from NC State. And doors will open elsewhere. Earlier this year, Rivers was interviewed for the head-coaching vacancy with the Bills. If Rivers turns the Wolfpack into a contender, other NFL teams inevitably will come calling.

#2711. Officials who were bumped by reconfigured Week 10 schedule won't lose pay

The four officials who worked two games in Week 10 got paid accordingly. The officials who found themselves without an assignment after the deck was reshuffled due to the ground stop won’t be docked.

NFL Referees Association executive director Scott Green tells PFT that the Collective Bargaining Agreement between the NFL and the NFLRA “requires that all active officials including anybody out with an injury are guaranteed 16 games of pay during the regular season regardless if they work all 16 games.”

Of course, it’s possible that the NFL will reconfigure upcoming assignments to balance out the total assignments, putting an official who was supposed to work a given weekend on a bye while assigning an official to work when the official was supposed to be off. (That’s not something Green said; it’s our own assessment of how the imbalance can be rectified.)

Hopefully, that won’t happen. Those who were supposed to work but didn’t should get credit for the game, since they were prepared to show up and do the job. And those who worked when they weren’t supposed to shouldn’t lose the opportunity to work their otherwise normal schedule — unless they choose that.

And the four who worked two games in one day should absolutely get some sort of a bonus. As Green said, "[T]hat’s pretty remarkable as you are generally pretty wiped out both mentally and physically after working a game.”

The fact that Don Willard, Terry Killens, Jabir Walker, and Justin Larrew went above and beyond the call makes a reward not just appropriate but necessary.

#2710. Knicks’ Karl-Anthony Towns acting like ‘second coach’ sign of fixed Mike Brown partnership

TORONTO — A once rocky partnership has evolved into a trusting and collaborative relationship between Karl-Anthony Towns and Mike Brown.

Towns revealed Tuesday that Brown has been embracing his suggestions "like I'm a second coach," which is a residual benefit of their success in the 2026 postseason.

"It's more familiarity with the system and building that trust with Mike where he can trust me with things I see and say, which started in the playoffs," Towns said before scoring 16 points and grabbing 16 rebounds in the Knicks' 111-102 win over the Raptors on Wednesday night. "So building off that this year. Having more input and him hearing me out."

Towns said he and the coach collaborate "a lot" during games.

"Him trusting me like a second coach. Me and him are always coaching with each other in the game, looking at game plans and plays and maybe ideologies and things I see that can help our team win, especially in close games," Towns said. "Working with him and coaching on the floor. It's been great. It's been fun."

Karl-Anthony Towns looks to make a play as Scottie Barnes defends during the Knicks' 111-102 win over the Raptors on Nov. 17, 2026, in Toronto. NBAE via Getty Images

It wasn't always so harmonious between the coach and All-Star center. When Brown took over before last season, Towns' production plummeted and it was clear the player-coach dynamic was off-kilter.

As Towns said multiple times during last season's struggles, "The system is different." Towns also declared during the 2025 training camp that he didn't know his role. It got messier by midseason, when Brown called out Towns in January for not recovering on defense after falling on a layup attempt.

"When you fall down, you've got to get up and got to sprint down the floor," Brown said at the time. "And even when you're the last guy down the floor, you've got to get down there just in case there's a long rebound. There was no urgency."

But then Brown placed Towns as an offensive hub in the playoffs, and the Knicks raised a banner because, among other reasons, the adjustment worked spectacularly.

This season, Towns is off to the best start on the roster with averages of 21.6 points and 13.6 rebounds entering Tuesday's game against the Raptors. He was shooting an efficient 53.4 percent from the field, and 39.7 percent from beyond the arc — both dramatic improvements from last season.

Mike Brown shouts out instructions on the baseline during the fourth quarter of the Knicks' loss to the Cavaliers on Nov. 11, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

Not coincidentally, he's in a better place with his head coach.

"We just grew. We've grown with each other," Towns said. "And obviously there's trust that has to be established and both of us trust each other a lot. And the last playoff run showed it. So just building off that trust and the relationship that was built in the playoffs and having it make our team better this year."

Brown agreed they're in a better place and he's given KAT ownership of the adjustments.

"It's just part of being around each other," Brown said. "You hope your relationship with everybody grows. That's not gonna always happen but you hope it does. KAT's got a really good feel of how to play the game. Obviously he's a perennial All-Star. So those things you've always got to take into account when it comes to trying to listen or try to adapt more to what their style of play is.

"As a head coach — come in, it's your first year, and you have an idea what you want to do. Sometimes it doesn't fit. Offensively, or defensively. And that's when you've got to pivot. And so we kept pivoting and pivoting and building until we found a nice little groove. We feel pretty good about each other right now."

#2709. Police issue arrest warrants for August burglary at Max Iheanachor's home

In August, thieves targeted the home of Steelers rookie first-round tackle Max Iheanachor while he was in Buffalo for a preseason game. Police have now issued arrest warrants in connection with the crime.

Via Mike DeFabo of The Athletic, the warrants target a trio of Los Angeles residents: Winter Brantley, 25, Andre Castle Jr., 23, and Lamar Stewart, 45.

They face charges of burglary, criminal trespassing, theft by unlawful taking, receiving stolen property, and conspiracy. Pittsburgh authorities are working with officials in California to apprehend the suspects.

Winter Brantley is the sister of Iheanachor’s roommate, Ashley Brantley, per police records.

The thieves allegedly absconded with roughly $150,000 to $200,000 in jewelry and designer bags. Per DeFabo, the criminal complaints reveal that police used video evidence from the Pittsburgh International Airport, security camera footage from neighbors, cell phone communications, cell phone location history, rental car billing information, and United Airlines flight information to identify the suspects.

Iheanachor has appeared in all nine regular-season games for the Steelers, with seven starts.

#2707. Victor Wembanyama throws down two self-alley-oop dunks off backboard in first quarter vs. Pistons

Victor Wembanyama's combination of height, length, athleticism and ingenuity allows him to not only do things on the basketball court that few other players could even dream of — to consistently, and at the NBA level, make competing against the best opposition in the world look like a dad lording over his elementary schooler in the driveway.

On Tuesday, against the Detroit Pistons — a team that won 60 games last season, a team that entered their matchup at 8-4, with the NBA's No. 6 defense — the San Antonio Spurs superstar center did that twice.

Just over a minute into the game, the 7-foot-4 MVP candidate crossed half-court, trailing the play as teammate Stephon Castle surveyed the floor. Wembanyama took a pass from Castle, and then a screen from point guard De'Aaron Fox, and ran an inverted pick-and-roll, driving to his right toward the rim.

Pistons guard Daniss Jenkins swiped at the ball as Wembanyama approached. There was just one problem: Jenkins is 6-foot-4, and Wembanyama is a literal foot taller than that. The Frenchman blithely windmill-gathered the ball over Jenkins' head without breaking his stride and got two feet in the paint before getting airborne.

At that point, Detroit defensive ace Ausar Thompson — who finished third last season in Defensive Player of the Year voting, behind winner Wembanyama and runner-up Chet Holmgren — soared in from the weak side, intent on protecting the rim from encroaching alien threats. This is the kind of thing that might unnerve most drivers.

Wembanyama, however, is not most drivers:

He floated the ball over the outstretched arm of Thompson and off the backboard with his right hand, landed in the restricted area, collected the carom with his left hand, and tomahawked it home. Two points, with a little bit of panache.

Well, evidently, Big Vic liked the feeling of self-containment inherent in that sort of farm-to-table approach to bucket-getting … because about five minutes later, he decided to do it again:

Wembanyama drove from the perimeter on John Collins, who's been starting at center for the Pistons with All-NBA pivot Jalen Duren sidelined with an injury. Wemby planted his left foot, spun back to his right into the paint, and once again tossed the ball off the backboard. The 6-foot-9 Collins, with inside position, went up to try to collect the rebound. Wembanyama, wielding the dual benefits of knowing what he had in mind before he'd ever let the ball go and significantly longer arms than his opponent, just reached up over the top of him, grabbed the ball, and dunked it. Again.

Once upon a time, an NBA player throwing an alley-oop to himself off the backboard was an exceedingly rare and remarkable thing. For the exceedingly rare and remarkable Wembanyama, though, the self-lob dunk has become downright common — to the point that, when he does it twice in a six-minute span, the only appropriate response is, "It's ridiculous that that's no longer ridiculous." (Other reasonable responses are inappropriate, because they feature expletives.)

→ Baca artikel asli

#2706. NFL Power Rankings entering Week 11: Can the Browns keep winning in dramatic fashion?

NFC East: Cowboys | Giants | Eagles | Commanders NFC North: Bears | Lions | Packers | Vikings NFC South: Falcons | Panthers | Saints | Buccaneers NFC West: Cardinals | Rams | 49ers | Seahawks

AFC East: Bills | Dolphins | Patriots | Jets AFC North: Ravens | Bengals | Browns | Steelers AFC South: Texans | Colts | Jaguars | Titans AFC West: Broncos | Chiefs | Raiders | Chargers

The Cleveland Browns have been through this type of season before. It has just been a long time.

In 1979, the Browns had seven fourth-quarter drives to win games. In the 1980 season they were 11-5 and nine of the wins came by one score. That group got the nickname of the Kardiac Kids. Brian Sipe became a Cleveland legend, and won the 1980 NFL MVP award.

The 2026 Browns aren't at that level yet, but they're getting close. After rallying to beat the Houston Texans on a touchdown with 53 seconds left, the Browns are the first team in NFL history to win each of its first six games by four or fewer points, according to ESPN Insights. Every one of those wins has included a game-winning drive in the fourth quarter. It has been a magical, improbable run.

"We're going to figure something out about starting a hell of a lot faster," Browns head coach Todd Monken said, according to the team's transcripts. "But our guys continue to fight, and they battle, and they don't, they don't blink. Which is probably more important than starting fast, is when you have guys that continue to fight."

Whether it's sustainable might be irrelevant. Suddenly, the 6-4 Browns are in great shape to make the playoffs.

With the New England Patriots likely in for a big drop after Drake Maye's ankle injury and the Browns knocking the Texans back while also clinching the head-to-head tiebreaker, Cleveland's playoff hopes got a lot brighter Sunday. There was another assist on Monday night, when the previously winless Chargers beat the Ravens, knocking Baltimore back to 4-6. The Browns would be the seventh seed if the season ended today and they're a game up on any other team in the running. It's hard to find a team behind them — Patriots, Ravens, Raiders, Texans — that's going to make a big push. Three of those teams' quarterbacks are either injured or traded and 3-6 Houston is miles behind now because of the tiebreaker.

In Week 1, the Browns were blown out 34-10 by the Jaguars and there were plenty of jokes about how they were already tanking for a quarterback. Now Cleveland is 6-4 heading into a well deserved bye week.

"Isn't that cool? Isn't that awesome?" Monken said. "I mean, holy s***. There's no other way to put it, man."

There are still seven games to go and like Monken said, the Browns have to figure out how to play better in the first three quarters of games to win enough to get a playoff berth. But they have already stacked six wins and are in the clear lead for a wild-card spot. Cleveland fans couldn't have expected a playoff berth, but a fun start to the season has the Browns well within range.

Here are the NFL power rankings after Week 10:

One of the signs of a good team is the ability to blow out bad teams. The Jaguars' 40-23 win over the Titans was their fifth win this season by 17 or more points, and it came with Jacksonville gambling that it could give trade additions defensive tackle Jordan Davis and safety Jessie Bates III the week off. In more than half of Jacksonville's games, it has throttled its opponent. The Jags' point differential of +125 is 37 points better than the Bills, who are second.

The Jaguars haven't played a tough schedule, but it actually gets easier. Their remaining games against teams with a winning record come against the Giants, Bears and Steelers. The Jaguars are going to put up a very good record and are just hoping the other top teams in the AFC, most notably Buffalo, lose some.

The 49ers cooked the Cowboys' defense, putting up 46 points and 584 yards. Dallas' defense is dreadful but it's still hard to pile up close to 600 yards in any NFL game.

The way the 49ers have climbed to the top of the NFL in yards per game is worth studying. Brock Purdy was famously Mr. Irrelevant in the draft and now he's an MVP candidate. Christian McCaffrey was at a point in which age and touches should have started to chip away at his effectiveness, but he is tied for the NFL lead with 13 total touchdowns. Draft experts laughed at the Kaelon Black pick in the third round; he's one of the best backup running backs in the league. Deebo Samuel Sr.'s career looked like it might be over after last season, but he has five touchdowns in six games. The 49ers had to give up only a sixth-round draft pick for Keenan Allen in a trade and he had five catches for 61 yards on Sunday.

The conclusion: Kyle Shanahan is your NFL Coach of the Year.

Beating the Jets doesn't seem to be that impressive, but it was to Bills head coach Joe Brady. His explanation made sense once he laid out that Buffalo had short rest before a road game after beating a great Vikings defense in Minneapolis last Monday night.

"This is the best win we've had this season," Brady said, via the team's site.

"We're getting back real early Tuesday morning [from Minnesota] to be able to reset and get their bodies ready to go to go back on the road."

It absolutely was impressive for the Bills to win 31-12 in a letdown spot, no matter the competition. The Bills host the Dolphins this weekend before a big showdown against the Chiefs on Thanksgiving.

The Bengals' performance Sunday night might have been the most impressive of the NFL season. Critics doubted the Steelers' 6-2 start, but they ranked sixth in DVOA coming into Sunday night's game. And the Bengals made them look like a bottom-feeding Conference USA opponent in town for homecoming. The Bengals outgained the Steelers 337-40 in the first half, which shouldn't happen against any competent NFL team. Cincinnati looked like a legitimate Super Bowl contender. It now ranks No. 1 in DVOA this season.

Joe Burrow had 302 passing yards and three touchdowns by halftime. If the Steelers had kept it competitive, Burrow might have challenged Norm Van Brocklin's longstanding NFL record of 554 passing yards in a game. Burrow finished with 409 passing yards. When asked what kind of quarterback he was on Sunday night, Burrow said, "A great one." The Bengals are feeling good, and rightfully so. The biggest key might be the offensive line, which allowed one sack on 46 dropbacks from Burrow.

"They've been unbelievable," Burrow said, via the team's transcripts.

The Bears didn't do anything wrong on their bye week. They didn't "move down." The Bengals just moved up after one of the most impressive games of the season.

Lingering for the Bears is their home defeat to the Vikings in Week 2. They lost 9-3, and based on how the Vikings looked with Kirk Cousins in Week 10, that could be a problem. The Bears play at Minnesota in Week 18 and that's looking like an early candidate to be flexed to the final Sunday night slot.

Chicago is 0-2 in the division (Minnesota is 3-1) and that could also be bad for tiebreakers. The Lions suffered a massive injury to Aidan Hutchinson on Sunday and the Packers lost ground with a bad loss, so the NFC North could evolve into a two-team race. And the Bears might need to win in Minneapolis in Week 18 to take the division.

The Vikings blew out the Packers without three key players: linebacker Blake Cashman, receiver Jordan Addison and tight end T.J. Hockenson. Kirk Cousins is 38 and there should be skepticism whether he can turn the Vikings into a Super Bowl threat, but everything looked much better in Week 10. And receiver Justin Jefferson had a surprisingly self-aware comment on where the Vikings stand.

"All we as an offense have to do is be mediocre at this point," Jefferson said, via The Athletic. "The defense is creating so much pressure."

He's not wrong. The Vikings were fierce on Sunday, pressuring Jordan Love 22 times on 35 dropbacks, according to Pro Football Focus. Love was sacked eight times. The Vikings weren't tremendous on offense with 312 yards, but as Jefferson pointed out, that might be enough.

The Chiefs also didn't do anything to "move down" in a thorough 35-13 win over the Falcons. The Vikings needed to be adjusted after seeing them play well with Kirk Cousins. At this point it's fair to wonder if the Chiefs are being underrated, which is strange for a team that has been in the spotlight so much over the Patrick Mahomes era. There isn't much evidence to suggest they should be higher. They're eighth in DVOA. They're sixth in Super Bowl odds. They're sixth in offensive and defensive EPA. They're clearly good, but are they Super Bowl contender good?

A big piece of that answer could be Josh Downs. He had four catches for 53 yards and a touchdown just five days after he was traded to the Chiefs. Xavier Worthy was shut out, and we can finally close the book on him making any meaningful impact. It's just not going to happen. The Chiefs need something extra in the offense, and perhaps it's Downs, who always seemed to have more potential and the Colts couldn't unlock it. Mahomes had 348 passing yards and five touchdowns on Sunday and perhaps that's a sign of a hot streak coming.

It's hard to find an advanced metric that indicates the Broncos should be higher than this. They're 14th in point differential, ninth in DVOA, 15th in net EPA as of last week and also a stunning 30th in yards per game on offense and 27th in yards allowed per game on defense. Their Super Bowl odds are tied for ninth best, for what that's worth. But here's why that doesn't matter: Denver is 7-2 after stacking (close) wins and has the easiest remaining schedule in the NFL, via DVOA, after playing the hardest schedule to this point in the season.

Here's what Denver has coming up: vs. Raiders, at Steelers, vs. Dolphins, at Jets, at Raiders, vs. Bills, at Patriots, vs. Chargers. That's two playoff contenders (assuming Drake Maye's injury sinks the Patriots) of eight remaining games. Like last season, the Broncos could end up with 13 or 14 wins and an army of naysayers wondering how good they really are.

The Sam Darnold/Drew Lock debate is flawed. Lock's three starts came twice against the Cardinals and vs. the Raiders. Arizona and Las Vegas have two of the worst defenses in the NFL. Still, Lock has played well and Darnold has been prone to turnovers. Lock hasn't thrown an interception and Darnold has nine despite missing three games and almost all of a fourth. But there isn't any indication from Seahawks head coach Mike Macdonald that he's considering a change.

"Drew's been fantastic," Macdonald said Monday on Seattle Sports 710, via the Seattle Times. "Drew's been phenomenal. But Sam's our quarterback."

That's the correct answer. Lock has played well and if Darnold continues to struggle, the Seahawks can turn to him later in the season. It would be premature to make a switch based on three games against bad opponents.

The Rams have won four in a row. They're still more likely than not to miss the playoffs — 44.2% chance to make it, via DVOA — because they have by far the NFL's toughest remaining schedule. But the Rams played well, albeit against a bad Cardinals team using rookie quarterback Carson Beck in his first career start. The Rams won 34-6 by running it 46 times for 202 yards. Konata Mumpfield provided some hope at receiver with Puka Nacua and Davante Adams out, with three catches for 81 yards. L.A.'s defense limited Arizona to six first downs.

It's still a hard path to the playoffs because of the schedule and injuries, but the Rams are hanging around.

"I think it's really tested our team," head coach Sean McVay said of having to find injury replacements. "But it's also shown there's a lot of guys that are capable of, hey let's instill belief and then let's go have that confidence earned through evidence."

The Lions' season changed with the reported ACL injury to defensive end Aidan Hutchinson. They were already without linebacker Jack Campbell, arguably the second-best player on the defense, and Detroit lost its unquestioned star because of a sack celebration. That's brutal. Derrick Moore and DJ Wonnum are capable vets and will fill in, but they're nowhere near Hutchinson's level.

"Any time you lose a player of his caliber, the type of teammate he is — he's a captain of ours — that doesn't make things easy," Lions head coach Dan Campbell said, according to NFL.com.

The Lions won on Sunday without Hutchinson for most of the game to improve to 7-2, and perhaps they have enough wins banked to make the playoffs in what will be a difficult wild-card race. But their hopes of being a Super Bowl contender are likely dashed without their two best defensive players.

The Packers' bye comes at a good time. They were playing very well before Sunday, when they were crushed at home by the Vikings. The break gives the Packers a chance to regroup, and also perhaps get Micah Parsons back for a game against the Rams the day before Thanksgiving. The Packers should rightly believe that Parsons will transform their team upon his return.

But there are concerns after Sunday. The offensive line was in shambles against the Vikings after showing improvement over a few weeks. The Vikings are a tough matchup, but Jordan Love can't get hit 12 times. The running game also disappeared without Josh Jacobs as Green Bay had just 56 yards on 17 carries. There are some things to clean up before a massive game in the wild-card race against the Rams.

Was Sunday's outing just a bad night against a hot Bengals team or a bad sign for the Steelers going forward? It's OK to excuse it as a one-off. Before the 38-18 blowout loss, the Steelers were 6-2 and showing improvement. It's also OK to worry that Pittsburgh was exposed, though Aaron Rodgers didn't think so when asked if the loss changes the season's trajectory.

"That's very dramatic," Rodgers said, via the team's site. "I've been a part of this league for a long time. There's big wins, there's tough losses, so we'll deal with this properly."

One thing that is an issue for the Steelers is their receiver room. DK Metcalf continued to have minimal impact, as he was held without a catch before garbage time in the fourth quarter. Germie Bernard was blanked and has just 57 yards in Pittsburgh's last three games. No other healthy receiver on the roster has more than 39 yards this season. Roman Wilson and Michael Pittman Jr. could both return in the next couple weeks, and that is badly needed.

One developing story is what to do with Deshaun Watson. Those who log quarterback wins will say he is 6-3 this season. That's misguided, but many people follow that logic. Watson is 20th in adjusted EPA per play and he has a middling 89.1 passer rating. That's 20th among quarterbacks with at least 100 attempts. The Browns have had some fun comeback wins and it will be interesting to see if Cleveland overreacts to that and extends Watson, whose contract has been one of the worst in the NFL.

Certainly, the Browns would rather not have to come back and win every game, as they've been doing.

"I wish collectively him and I together would play better early and coach better early and we didn't have to get to this," Browns head coach Todd Monken said, via the team's transcripts. "And it's not just been them. It's been our team, last week the same way."

The Buccaneers looked to have a tough four-game stretch after the bye when the schedule came out. It has gotten much easier. Their next four opponents are the Lions (lost Aidan Hutchinson and Jack Campbell to injuries between Weeks 8 and 10), Panthers (lost three in a row before a win Sunday), Chargers (a stunningly bad start to the season) and Ravens (lost quarterbacks Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley to injuries). That's why forecasting schedule strength can be difficult.

Now the Buccaneers have to take advantage of their good fortune. They're up to a 60.9% chance to win the division, via DVOA, in part because their defense ranks first in the NFL in that metric and they have the third-easiest remaining schedule.

The Giants are still in fine shape to win the NFC East because the Cowboys seem incapable of getting a stop on defense and the Commanders are flawed too. But blowing Thursday night's game against Washington keeps the pressure on them. The Giants had two chances to put the game away with the lead but went three-and-out each time. Then J.J. McCarthy threw an interception to end the game.

Otherwise, McCarthy played pretty well. He was 23-of-36 passing for 293 yards. Since Week 6, which was his first significant playing time with the Giants, he's 10th in adjusted EPA per play among quarterbacks with at least 100 plays. He hasn't been perfect but he's still young, and joining a team in-season and becoming a starter is a tough assignment.

"I thought he played very well," Giants head coach John Harbaugh said after the loss to the Commanders, according to the team's transcripts. "I mean, there were some plays made, he made some throws, scrambled. I thought J.J. played very well."

The Panthers paid edge rusher Jaelan Phillips $120 million over four years, and at that price point Phillips should be one of the biggest impact players in the NFL. On Sunday, he finally was. Phillips had three tackles for loss, including a sack, and the Panthers tied their season best by allowing 222 yards in a win over the Saints.

The Panthers' defense, along with a huge game from running back Chuba Hubbard, was the difference in a major victory in the NFC South race. It was Phillips' first game back after missing three due to a hamstring injury.

"Obviously there's been a lot of adversity, a lot of change, things happening, and we deserve some good things happening to us, so it was really good to get out there and play well." Phillips said, via the team's site.

Sunday was the fifth time this season that the Texans have given up a drive in the final minutes of regulation or overtime to lose a game. Houston was supposed to be one of the best defenses in the NFL. The Texans were second in EPA per play allowed last season. This season they're 20th, despite having mostly the same personnel and coaches.

While defensive success can fluctuate from season to season more than offensive success, the Texans' decline is perplexing. If Houston finished just two of those five games, it would be above .500.

"We have to play better — run defense, have to tackle better," head coach DeMeco Ryans said, via Texans Wire.

Fernando Mendoza made plenty of mistakes in his first NFL start, but he faced a Seattle defense that is among the top three in the NFL. Mendoza was 17-of-29 passing for 143 yards, and the biggest issue was that he took six sacks.

Mendoza will get better, though his next two matchups are also tough at Denver and at Cleveland. The schedule will get easier, but one of the concerns of the Kirk Cousins trade was shoving Mendoza into the lineup during a very difficult stretch. That was evident during a tough matchup against Seattle.

"It's good to get punched in the face, because now it's a great opportunity to grow and learn and to really unveil all the deficiencies that I have in my game, as a future hopeful leader of this offense," Mendoza said, via the Raiders' site.

Linebacker Sonny Styles is going to make a case for Defensive Rookie of the Year. Against the Giants, the seventh overall pick of the draft had 13 tackles. His biggest tackle came on a third-and-3 right after the two-minute warning when he shot through for a tackle for loss on a J.J. McCarthy designed quarterback run. Styles read that play because the Giants had run it in Week 5, which is high-level awareness from a rookie.

That tackle allowed Washington to get the ball back, and Jayden Daniels led a game-winning drive that kept the Commanders' season alive. Styles is up to 80 tackles on the season and he is used often as a blitzer. He has one interception and one sack. He is looking like a great first-round pick.

The Patriots took a big drop in the rankings and it might not be far enough. Drake Maye suffered what the Patriots fear is a fractured ankle, per ESPN. At the very least he'll be out a while and the most likely outcome seems to be that his season is over.

That turns the offense over to Tommy DeVito. It has been a long time since he had a few decent games as a rookie for the Giants in 2023 and became a fleeting cult figure. He looked entirely overmatched Sunday after Maye left the game. He had 81 yards on 24 attempts against a bad Lions defense. It's hard to see how the Patriots keep their season afloat without Maye, unless DeVito somehow plays way better than he did Sunday or the Patriots find some very unexpected help off the street.

There's not much more that can be said about the Cowboys' defense. Even noted Cowboys fan Post Malone was absolutely fed up. After allowing 584 yards in a 46-40 loss to the 49ers, Brian Schottenheimer made perhaps his harshest comments on the team since becoming head coach.

"I thought the second half was unacceptable," Schottenheimer said, according to the team's site. "Tackling was atrocious. We're going to do a deep dive and see why."

It's not like the Cowboys' offense has been the 1999 Rams or anything: fifth in points scored, eighth in yards gained, fifth in EPA per play and sixth in DVOA. That still should be enough to win a poor division. The defense is ruining the Cowboys' season, though DVOA still gives Dallas a 32.4% chance to win the NFC East.

The Ravens lost but can't put all the blame on quarterback injuries. Jacoby Brissett had 340 yards and mostly played well. But the running game was invisible on Monday night against the Chargers. Derrick Henry had just 10 yards on 10 carries. Baltimore's defense wasn't good enough, allowing Los Angeles to rush for a season-best 160 yards. The Ravens have lost five in a row and are in a bad place the rest of the season.

The AFC wild-card race is soft, but it's not like the Ravens are looking like much of a threat. They just lost a home game to a Chargers team that was 0-8 coming in. Even if Lamar Jackson heals quickly, it seems like there won't be any reason for him to return late in the season.

The Saints' next three games are at Chicago, at Cincinnati and vs. Green Bay. This could get bad for New Orleans, which is showing that last season's strong second half was a fluke fueled by a weak schedule. The Panthers have allowed less than 300 yards this season four times: vs. the Cooper Rush-led Falcons, vs. the Browns, vs. the Tanner McKee-led Eagles and on Sunday against the Saints. New Orleans had just 222 yards of offense. There were offensive line injuries but it was still a poor performance and a bad home loss.

"Yeah, [coach Kellen Moore] was upset, and rightfully so," Saints quarterback Tyler Shough said via the team's transcripts. "As players, I think we failed him. As coach, I think we had those moments where we had a couple third downs, and I could have found Chris [Olave] or made a play there. We had those opportunities, and he's obviously frustrated. We have to find a way to win that game."

If there was a positive it was seeing a healthy Jordyn Tyson, the Saints' first-round draft pick, grab 11 passes for 80 yards as Carolina focused its coverage on Olave.

In Weeks 3 and 4, with Michael Penix Jr. back in the lineup, the Falcons blew out the Packers and Saints and looked like a potential division winner. Other than those two games, the Falcons are 1-7. Whatever happened in those games was a complete outlier. Atlanta averaged 459 yards in those two games and hasn't been above 370 yards in any other game this season. Bijan Robinson hasn't rushed for more than 100 yards in a game since that Week 4 win at New Orleans.

On Sunday the defense completely let down, days after the team traded safety Jessie Bates III to the Jaguars. Patrick Mahomes had 348 yards and five touchdowns and Kansas City scored 35 points without running back Kenneth Walker III.

"All of this falls on me," Falcons head coach Kevin Stefanski said, via the team's site. "So, I'll take responsibility for everything that goes on in this football team. We all have to be better; that's the truth."

Everyone got way too excited for rookie quarterback Carson Beck because of one solid performance in the Hall of Fame Game. The overreaction to that was fierce. Beck, in his first game since then (he missed the rest of the preseason with a rib injury), had 108 yards and a 38.4 passer rating. Arizona had just six first downs and didn't score a touchdown. Beck had a tough matchup against the Rams and the Cardinals had many injuries on offense. Sunday was still a reality check. Beck could be a good player, but it's going to take time.

"It was a rough day, and that's probably a nice way of saying it, for the offense," Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur said, via the team's site, though he also said he "was impressed" with Beck.

The good news for Beck is the Cardinals aren't going anywhere and have no other option at quarterback the rest of the way. They'll get seven more games to evaluate Beck and he'll have some easier matchups. Arizona will know whether it has its quarterback for 2027 or needs to draft another one.

The Colts can win without their fans being conflicted because the team doesn't have its own 2027 first-round pick. Something that made no sense at the trade deadline and made less sense Sunday is why Indianapolis didn't deal away running back Jonathan Taylor. The Colts had reported offers of a second- and fifth-round pick for Taylor and they passed. Yet, the Colts split the backfield between Taylor and Seth McGowan again Sunday, with Taylor getting 16 carries and McGowan 12. The Colts could get a committee back for much cheaper than a second-round pick. As for the idea of not wearing out Taylor in a lost season, do they think they'll be a playoff contender next season? Seems unlikely.

The other part of the Colts' season that is hard to believe is how bad Daniel Jones has been. Jones had 108 yards and a 41.5 passer rating against a bad Dolphins defense. Jones has the lowest passer rating of any regular starting quarterback in the NFL this season, at 67. That's a 33-point drop from last season, which is stunning even accounting for injuries and trades.

Kyle McCord wasn't all that bad against the Colts in his first NFL start. He easily outplayed his counterpart, Daniel Jones. He was 17-of-32 passing for 209 yards and he ran for a touchdown. McCord also lost a fumble that was recovered in the end zone for a touchdown. Dolphins head coach Jeff Hafley said he was impressed with how McCord bounced back afterward.

"He didn't blink. He just stayed composed," Hafley said, according to the team's transcripts. "Coming out of the half, I mean, that thing could have gotten ugly and the game could have gotten away from us when he fumbled the ball and they scored, and he just kept responding the whole entire time."

McCord has at least a few more weeks to show what he can do in the NFL as Malik Willis is on injured reserve. With a little more improvement he can perhaps carve out a role as a reliable backup.

Cam Ward has improved, and Titans head coach Robert Saleh revealed Monday that the team gave Ward more responsibility at the line of scrimmage before Week 7. In the three games since, the Titans' scoring average has spiked from 14 points per game to 27.7, and that came against three good defenses (Browns, Bengals, Jaguars). Ward has three games this season with a passer rating above 83 and two of them came since the philosophical change.

The Titans are in the middle of a lost season, but a strong second half from Ward can offer renewed hope for the future.

"To be fair to Cam, we've said from the beginning we were going to give him every opportunity to prove that he is a franchise quarterback of the Tennessee Titans," Saleh said, via the team's site. "So, philosophically, the change was: How are we going to evaluate whether or not he is capable of being our franchise quarterback if we don't give him the opportunity to prove it? And I think over the last three weeks he has done that. Or, at least is doing it."

Not much has gone right for the Eagles this season, but the A.J. Brown trade looks to be a win for them. Brown has been stunningly bad for the Patriots. A high ankle sprain suffered in Week 1 has contributed to his quiet season. Overall, New England can't be happy to have just 11 catches and 128 yards from a receiver who will be 30 years old next season and cost the Patriots a 2028 first-round draft pick.

The Eagles are positioned well in the next two drafts, with extra first-round picks in 2027 and 2028 and extra second-round picks in 2028 and 2029, because of the trades of Jordan Davis, Cam Jurgens and Brown. That might not make the rest of this season any more palatable but Philadelphia should be able to reload quickly.

When Aaron Glenn said he was taking over defensive play-calling this season, he said calling plays was his "superpower." On Sunday, he gave up the "lead" play-calling duties to defensive coordinator Brian Duker, which practically means that Glenn is no longer the play-caller. Not that Jets fans needed another sign that Glenn won't be back in 2027 as New York's head coach, but that adds to the list.

The next big question will be when to flip the QB1 job to rookie Cade Klubnik from veteran Geno Smith. Klubnik got some playing time after Smith injured his ankle Sunday, and a 2-8 team should be thinking about getting a look at a rookie and benching a 36-year-old who will be a free agent after the season. Glenn said that's not a consideration yet.

"Geno's our starter," Glenn said Monday, via ESPN. "Come on now, let's not do that.

"I think Geno's had a really good year and it would be disrespectful to him to just [replace him]. I think we all understand that. I think we all know that he's our starter."

The Chargers got the win they needed to avoid a lot of annoying 0-17 questions. Let's not get too far ahead of ourselves; it was just one win, and on the whole, they've still been the NFL's worst team this season. But give the Chargers credit for a good effort. They figured out how to make the Ravens one-dimensional. Baltimore had 16 yards rushing on 15 carries. That forced Baltimore to lean too much on quarterback Jacoby Brissett, who was making his first Ravens start after being traded less than two weeks ago.

It was also encouraging to see Omarion Hampton emerge with a 105-yard rushing day. Justin Herbert completed only 12 passes but he didn't need to do much more because the running game was effective. Maybe the Chargers can build on their first win and have a respectable second half of the season.

#2703. Week 11 Chicago Bears power rankings roundup

The Chicago Bears are back in action following the bye week. It was great for the Bears to pause and have a chance to get healthy, but now is where the stakes are really going to go up.

The schedule is full of playoff-hopeful teams, and that includes four more games against the NFC North gauntlet.

Much is being made of Chicago's Super Bowl chances after their 7-2 start. Now they actually have to go out and prove it.

Let's see where the Bears rank heading into their final eight games.

Second-Half Outlook: Survive a brutal schedule down the stretch and hope that Jeffery Simmons continues to be a difference-maker

Once dubbed a prime regression candidate heading into the regular season, the Bears have survived without Caleb Williams for three games and somehow continue to play much better defense than the numbers suggest. Williams' Year 3 jump has been notable, even with a four-interception game in Week 9. The talent on offense is evident, and their ceiling feels safe with Ben Johnson calling plays. How far they go this season will come down to the defense, especially the secondary. We'll see if not making another move at the deadline comes back to bite them.

The Athletic – Chad Graff and Josh Kendall

Ben Johnson was bullish about his second-year wide receiver in the preseason, and we're seeing why. Burden is 16th in the league with 644 receiving yards, and 385 of those came in his last four games. From weeks 6 through 9, he had more receiving yards than all but five players in the league, and he got 23.5 percent of the Bears' passing targets. That last number may go up as the season progresses.

Bears (No. 5; 7-2): They can't afford to overlook the Saints, four days before a showdown with the Lions.

The Bears didn't do anything wrong on their bye week. They didn't "move down." The Bengals just moved up after one of the most impressive games of the season.

Lingering for the Bears is their home defeat to the Vikings in Week 2. They lost 9-3, and based on how the Vikings looked with Kirk Cousins in Week 10, that could be a problem. The Bears play at Minnesota in Week 18 and that's looking like an early candidate to be flexed to the final Sunday night slot.

Chicago is 0-2 in the division (Minnesota is 3-1) and that could also be bad for tiebreakers. The Lions suffered a massive injury to Aidan Hutchinson on Sunday and the Packers lost ground with a bad loss, so the NFC North could evolve into a two-team race. And the Bears might need to win in Minneapolis in Week 18 to take the division.

6. Chicago Bears (4) Having spent last week on a bye, the Bears didn't do anything wrong — they just got caught up in a Power Rankings shakeup, with a couple of impressive teams leap-frogging them. Last time out, Chicago earned a narrow win over Tampa Bay that exposed some weaknesses but also felt like an anomaly in the larger picture. I still have plenty of confidence in this team, but it has to navigate the incredibly competitive NFC North to fulfill its potential. The Bears need to come out of the break on the right foot, facing a three-week stretch that includes dates with Detroit and Jacksonville.

3. Chicago Bears (3): An offense that runs the ball more than any other in the league is about to get RB Kyle Monangai back − which is akin to making a trade deadline acquisition for free.

Non-QB MVP at midseason: RB D'Andre Swift

The Bears have maintained a top-five rushing offense (142 yards per game) despite a constant wave of injuries in the backfield. A big reason for that is the load Swift has carried in Kyle Monangai's five-game absence (toe/thumb). Swift has logged the third-most offensive snaps of any Bears skill player (63%), including 22 rushes against the Patriots and 27 at Seattle a week later. He ran in three second-half TDs against the Seahawks, has already reached a career high of 10 TDs and is five away from setting the franchise's single-season record. Despite dealing with injuries of his own, Swift has not missed a game. — Courtney Cronin

4. Chicago Bears (4) They entered their bye as one of the league's hottest teams. Does it stay that way? There is nothing to think it won't.

What do you think of this week's rankings? The idle week didn't hurt them much, if at all.

#2702. Kirk Cousins, Vikings reunion could be just getting started

Quarterback Kirk Cousins will spend the rest of the 2026 season with the Vikings. That may not be the end of Kirko 2.0.

The Vikings have already expressed interest in keeping Cousins beyond the current season, we’re told. As we understand it, the sentiment was articulated casually and in passing before his rousing debut at Lambeau Field. And it was more of an indication that there will be discussions.

Of course, there will be a greater motivation to do something, now that it’s clear that Cousins is exactly what the doctor ordered for an offense that was ready for a visit from Dr. Kevorkian.

Even though Cousins is under contract through 2028, the deal was structured to spark a release after one season, with Cousins getting a $10 million payment on the back end aimed at sticking the Falcons with $8.7 million in 2026.

A base salary in 2028 of $78.655 million becomes fully guaranteed on the fifth day of the 2027 league year, along with a $1.345 million base salary in 2027. Although that works out to $80 million over two seasons (which puts the average closer to the bottom of the veteran quarterback pay scale than the top), the current structure imposes a ridiculously high cash and cap obligation for 2028, when he will be 40.

So the deal will need to be revised. And Cousins will have leverage, if he continues to do what he did on Sunday.

That said, he possibly will realize that it’s not the time to maximize his leverage. He did that in 2024, when he opted for a multi-year financial commitment that he mistakenly viewed to be a multi-year playing commitment. And then the Falcons pulled the rug out from under him by drafting Michael Penix Jr. roughly six weeks after the ink dried on the new deal.

And, frankly, the Vikings could decide to devise a plan for swallowing the proverbial poison pill and paying $40 million per year for two more season. The impact of Cousins’s return on the Minnesota offense would justify trying to devise a solution.

The best result would be for the two sides to work something out, at least for 2027. After 2023, the Vikings wanted to take a year-to-year approach with Cousins. Cousins wanted something more than that — even though the Rams and Matthew Stafford have been using the year-to-year thing for a while. In hindsight, a compromise would have been much better for both sides.

That lesson from the past should inform the future. As one source put it, sometimes it takes a breakup for everyone to appreciate each other.

It’s clear that, one week after Cousins returned to Minnesota, both sides have significant appreciation for each other. Which gives both sides a very strong motivation to find a way to keep it going at least for 2027.

Unless, of course, the Vikings win the Super Bowl this year and Cousins decides to walk off into the sunset with the trophy that will fit in that spot in his house in Michigan he has saved for it.

That would throw the Minnesota quarterback position back into disarray for 2027. But if it comes with the first Super Bowl win in franchise history, the Vikings would take that.

#2701. Stephen A. Smith drops harsh truth bomb on Steph Curry’s NBA future

The Warriors are expected to be without Steph Curry for a month due to an oblique abdominal tear, and there's concern it could affect his long-term health.

Stephen A. Smith appeared on his podcast on Tuesday and said Curry's ongoing injuries, along with the cartilage damage in his right knee, could indicate his current contract could be his last.

Stephen A. Smith drops a harsh truth bomb for the future of Steph Curry and the Golden State Warriors. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"The greatest shooter God has ever created. Steph Curry says 'deteriorated cartilage' in his right knee and ongoing injuries have made him more aware that his NBA career could be nearing its end," Smith said. "If you didn't remember, he signed a two-year extension for $116 million."

"Steph Curry has been on the record saying that this may very well be his last contract. He's [38] years old," Smith said.

Smith said the possibility of this being Curry's final contract makes him "sad."

Steph Curry during pregame warmups for the Warriors. NBAE via Getty Images

Curry is in the midst of his 18th NBA season and is considered one of the game's greatest shooters of all time.

From beyond the arc, Curry has a 42.2 shooting percentage. He has 26,806 career points and is continuing to do it at a high level.

On Oct. 28, Curry cemented his name with LeBron James, by tying him at 15 games for 40-point performances past age 35.

Steph Curry celebrating during an NBA game for the Warriors. Getty Images

Before his injury, Curry appeared in 10 games for the Warriors and averaged 27.8 points per game, shooting 50% from the field, 45.7% from 3-point range and 98.7% from the free-throw line.

"I think this brother is a showman. He is a showstopper. He is the greatest marksman the game has ever seen. He's a paragon of virtue when it comes to professional athletes. Nobody conducts themselves better," Smith said. "He's just a special brother. I will tell you that."

#2700. Bud Dupree was a healthy scratch after making comments about Chargers' front office

One of the interesting, on the surface, storylines arising from Monday night’s win by the Chargers over the Ravens came from the fact that L.A. linebacker Bud Dupree was a healthy scratch for the game — three days after he made some pointed comments about the team’s front office.

Did his comments help? Surely not. But this wasn’t a team abruptly shutting down a core member of the defense. It was the next logical step for a former first-round pick in 2015 who has started only five games in three seasons with the Chargers.

#2697. Knicks 111, Raptors 102: Scenes from a mirror game

What a weird one. The Knicks (8-6) beat the Raptors (5-9) on Sunday and stayed in town for a rematch two days later; in some ways, it felt like watching a replay of the first game. The Knicks lost the first frame by six, outscored the Dinos by 10 in the second, lost the third quarter by seven, and finally got straight in the fourth. Winning the frame 31-19 thanks to great play from the usual suspects (Captain Jalen Brunson, Karl-Anthony Towns, Landry Shamet, and OG Anunoby), New York hit the gas in crunch time to seal the deal, 111-102.

When these teams last met, New York needed a late rally to escape a 14-point hole and win in overtime. They coughed up the rock 18 times, tying their season-worst total. It wasn't pretty until late. According to the broadcast, Scottie Barnes (who scored 27 in the loss) credited Mikal Bridges specifically for dialing up the defensive intensity in the fourth quarter. Bridges has logged just three double-digit point games this season, which is less than ideal for a starter making $33.4 M, but when he applies the defensive screws, it makes up for his sins on the offensive side. The 30-year-old wing had his number called first tonight. He repaid the privilege by fighting for a contested foul-line jumper and scored seven points in his first seven minutes.

On the whole, however, his team had their difficulties through the first quarter. After scoring the first six points of the night, they watched nine straight points go right by them. The visitors outrebounded the Raps by a factor of two (good), but failed to capitalize on many of those second chances (less good). Blame their 39% shooting from the field and 29% from the perimeter (very not good). Meanwhile, the home team hit 63% of their first-quarter attempts and continued to dominate their guests in the paint. In their last tilt, the Dinos outscored them in the paint 52-36, and they looked ready to have a similar result. Score after one: 29-23.

Shades of a game just past haunted the 'Bockers in the second quarter, as Toronto stretched its lead to 13 points by the 5:38 mark. Ja'Kobe Walter did much of the damage, scoring eight points in a little over a minute, including two three-pointers, while Jamal Shead (who impressed on Sunday) repeatedly found open teammates for five assists. OAKAAKs RJ Barrett and Immanuel Quickley combined for a three-pointer, a layup, and a floater early in the period.

New York struggled to generate consistent offense. Bridges whiffed on a pair of threes, and Jordan Clarkson contributed little beyond an offensive foul and turnover. OG Anunoby provided some excitement with a dunk, two blocks, and three free throws, but the offense was flowing like ketchup from a bottle.

The Knicks finally found their footing. Josh Hart and Landry Shamet hit longalls, fueling a 9-0 run that cut Toronto's lead to 48-44. Towns then took over inside, smashing the offensive glass, scoring on a putback, a floater, and a dunk. The Raps are familiar with scoring droughts, and they slipped and tripped while the good guys rattled off a 19-2 run to end the half, 54-50.

By intermission, both teams were shooting 29% from downtown, but the Knicks were surviving inside, now outscoring the Raptors by four in the paint. They dominated the glass, 24-13, and turned the ball over only four times—although Toronto was almost perfect, committing just one. Through the half, Towns and RJ Barrett had scored 11 apiece.

Knicks coach Mike Brown is fortunate to have a full stable of guards. Of late, he's favored Tyler Kolek over Jose Alvarado, but tonight, after Kolek missed 3-of-4 from the field and offset his one dime with a turnover, Brown paired Alvarado with Brunson in the backcourt for a productive stretch in the third frame.

Unfortunately for our guys, the quarter was on the whole a more disappointing affair. Walter continued to shoot flames from his fingers (four triples and 12 points in the period), and Barrett added four points and some dimes. Brunson scored half of the Knicks' total in the third, as they were outscored 33-26. Bridges lent a layup and a three, but was also the team's worst -19 plus/minus. Josh Hart, with five points so far, was +15. You figure it out. The Knicks trailed 83-80 going into the final frame.

As on Sunday, so on Tuesday. The Knicks, mostly sleepy for most of the night, released a lion-like yawn and rose from their slumber to start the fourth with a 10-3 run. On the floor at the start of that good stuff were Kolek, Shamet, Hart, Anunoby, and Towns, with Jordan Clarkson spelling Anunoby a few minutes in. Redeeming himself for the iffy first half, Kolek was cooking—he had an especially beautiful behind-the-head pass from the paint to Shamet, who side-stepped a defender for a practice swish. We're salivating already at the idea of having Kolek and Alvarado off the bench in the playoffs.

Ha. Nevermind. After scoring 11 straight points, they took a break and let Toronto go on a 12-4 run. Quickley started that with a trey and Barrett ended it with another. At the four-minute mark, Anunoby netted a 28-footer (his first) to knot the score at 98. Then it was tied at 100. And yet again, the Americans and Canadians headed into crunch time with a very real threat of another overtime. At 1:36—102-102, thanks to a Barrett layup.

During the fourth, Walter was driving to the cup when the most unlikely of blocks occurred:

KAT dimed Cap for a bomb with 1:25 left to make it 105-102. On the shot, Walter was given a flagrant foul for violating Jalen's landing space (for once, we disagree with the call, but shhh). Brunson's face showed pain, but he walked it off and converted the four-point play. Toronto would not score again (Boyles fouled Shamet, Barrett missed a jumper, and Quickley lost the ball to Anunoby), and successfully completed their 15th straight loss to the Knicks. Well done, lads!

New York travels to Minnesota for a tussle with the Timberwolves. Mo is applying a second coat to your recap. Safe travels, Knickerbockers.
